, we have shown that the inverse-gamma distribution is a conjugate prior to the magnitude of covariance matrix of multivariate Gaussian distribution. A generalization to this is the inverse-Wishart distribution which is a conjugate prior to the full covariance matrix of multivariate Gaussian distribution. That is, the inverse-Wishart distribution is a probability distribution of random positive definite matrices that is used to model random covariance matrices.

Before the discussion about inverse-Wishart distribution. We shall notice that it derives from the Wishart distribution.
(
Anderson 2003
)
has said “The Wishart distribution ranks next to the (muiltivariate) normal distribution in order of importance and usefulness in multivariate statistics”.

Definition 13

(Wishart Distribution)
.

A random symmetric positive definite matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\in\real^{D\times D}
is said to follow the Wishart distribution with parameter
\bm{M}_{0}
and
\nu_{0}
if

\displaystyle\mathrm{Wi}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{M}_{0}},\nu_{0})

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}|^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{\nu_{0}-D-1}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{M}_{0}^{-1}})\right)

\displaystyle\times\left[2^{\frac{\nu_{0}D}{2}}\pi^{D(D-1)/4}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}|\bm{M}_{0}|^{\nu_{0}/2}}\prod_{d=1}^{D}\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+1-d}{2})\right]^{-1},

where
\nu_{0}>D
and
\bm{M}_{0}
is a
D\times D
symmetric positive definite matrix, and
|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}|=\det(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
.
The
\nu_{0}
is called the
number of degrees of freedom
, and
\bm{M}_{0}
is called the
scale matrix
.
And it is denoted by
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\sim\mathrm{W}(\bm{M}_{0},\nu_{0})
.
The mean and variance of Wishart distribution are given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}]

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}\bm{M}_{0},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{i,j}]

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}(m_{i,j}^{2}+m_{i,i}m_{j,j}),

where
m_{i,j}
is the
i
-th row
j
-th column element of
\bm{M}_{0}
.

An interpretation of the Wishart distribution is as follows. Suppose we sample i.i.d.,
\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{\nu_{0}}
from
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\bm{M}_{0})
. The sum of squares matrix of the collection of multivariate vectors is given by

\sum_{i=1}^{\nu_{0}}\bm{z}_{i}\bm{z}_{i}^{\top}=\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z},

where
\bm{Z}
is the
\nu_{0}\times D
matrix whose
i
-th row is
\bm{z}_{i}^{\top}
. It is trivial that
\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}
is positive semidefinite and symmetric. If
\nu_{0}>D
and the
\bm{z}_{i}
’s are linearly independent, then
\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}
will be positive definite and symmetric. That is
\bm{Z}\bm{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
only happens when
\bm{x}=\boldsymbol{0}
. We can repeat over and over again, generating matrices
\bm{Z}_{1}^{\top}\bm{Z}_{1},\bm{Z}_{2}^{\top}\bm{Z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{Z}_{l}^{\top}\bm{Z}_{l}
. The population distribution of these matrices has a Wishart distribution with parameters
(\bm{M}_{0},\nu_{0})
. By definition,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu_{0}}\bm{z}_{i}\bm{z}_{i}^{\top}

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{E}[\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{Z}]=\mathrm{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\nu_{0}}\bm{z}_{i}\bm{z}_{i}^{\top}\right]=\nu_{0}\mathrm{E}[\bm{z}_{i}\bm{z}_{i}^{\top}]=\nu_{0}\bm{M}_{0}.

When
D=1
, this reduces to the case that if
z
is a mean-zero univariate normal random variable, then
z^{2}
is a Gamma random variable. To be specific,

\mathrm{suppose}\qquad z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,a),\qquad\mathrm{then}\qquad z^{2}\sim\mathrm{Ga}(a/2,1/2).

Just like the relationship between inverse-Gamma distribution and Gamma distribution that if
x\sim\mathrm{Ga}(r,\lambda)
, then
y=\frac{1}{x}\sim\mathrm{IG}(r,\lambda)
. There is a similar connection between the inverse-Wishart distribution and Wishart-distribution.

Since we often use the inverse-Wishart distribution as a prior distribution for a covariance matrix, it is often useful to replace
\bm{M}_{0}
in the Wishart distribution by
\bm{S}_{0}=\bm{M}_{0}^{-1}
. This results in that
A random
D\times D
symmetric positive definite matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
has an
\mathrm{IW}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\bm{S}_{0},\nu_{0})
distribution if
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
has a Wishart
\mathrm{Wi}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}|\bm{M}_{0},\nu_{0})
distribution.

Definition 14

(Inverse-Wishart Distribution)
.

A random symmetric positive definite matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\in\real^{D\times D}
is said to follow the inverse-Wishart distribution with parameter
\bm{S}_{0}
and
\nu_{0}
if

\displaystyle\mathrm{IW}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{S}_{0}},\nu_{0})

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-\frac{\nu_{0}+D+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{S}_{0}})\right)

\displaystyle\times\left[2^{\frac{\nu_{0}D}{2}}\pi^{D(D-1)/4}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}|\bm{S}_{0}|^{-\nu_{0}/2}}\prod_{d=1}^{D}\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+1-d}{2})\right]^{-1},

where
\nu_{0}>D
and
\bm{S}_{0}
is a
D\times D
symmetric positive definite matrix, and
|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|=\det(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
.
The
\nu_{0}
is called the
number of degrees of freedom
, and
\bm{S}_{0}
is called the
scale matrix
.
And it is denoted by
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\sim\mathrm{IW}(\bm{S}_{0},\nu_{0})
.
The mean and mode of inverse-Wishart distribution is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}]

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}\bm{S}_{0}^{-1}=\nu_{0}\bm{M}_{0},

(25)

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu_{0}-D-1}\bm{S}_{0},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Mode}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu_{0}+D+1}\bm{S}_{0}.

Note that, sometimes, we replace
\bm{S}_{0}
by
\bm{M}_{0}=\bm{S}_{0}^{-1}
such that
\mathrm{E}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}]=\nu_{0}\bm{M}_{0}
which does not involve inverse of the matrix.

When
D=1
, the inverse-Wishart distribution reduces to the inverse Gamma such that
\frac{\nu_{0}}{2}=r
and
\frac{S_{0}}{2}=\lambda
, see Definition
:

\mathrm{IW}(y|S_{0},\nu_{0})=\mathrm{IG}(y|r,\lambda).

Note that the Wishart density is not simply the inverse-Wishart density with

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
replaced by
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}
. There is an additional factor of
|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-(D+1)}
. See
(
Anderson 2003
)
Theorem 7.7.1 that the Jacobian of the transformation
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}
is
|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-(D+1)}
. Substitution of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}
in the definition of Wishart distribution and multiply by
|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-(D+1)}
can yield the inverse-Wishart distribution.

We will see that a sample drawn from a normal-inverse-Wishart distribution gives a mean vector and a covariance matrix which can define a multivariate gaussian distribution. Separately, we can first sample a matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
from an inverse-Wishart distribution parameterized by (
\bm{S}_{0},\nu_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}
) which is called a semi-conjugate prior, and then sample a mean vector from a Gaussian distribution parameterized by (
\bm{m}_{0},\bm{V}_{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
).

8.4
Posterior distribution of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
: Separated view

Suppose the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is known in Equation (
), the likelihood is

\displaystyle\mathrm{\textbf{likelihood}}

\displaystyle=p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\mu})=\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-ND/2}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum^{N}_{n=1}(\mathbf{x}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\mu})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{x}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\right)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(N\overline{\bm{x}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}N\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\right).

The conjugate prior of the mean vector is also a Gaussian
p(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\bm{m}_{0},\bm{V}_{0})
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{\textbf{prior}}

\displaystyle=p(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\bm{m}_{0},\bm{V}_{0})

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-D/2}|\bm{V}_{0}|^{-1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{0})^{\top}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-D/2}|\bm{V}_{0}|^{-1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\bm{m}_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\bm{m}_{0}^{\top}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\bm{m}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\bm{m}_{0}\right).

By the Bayes’ theorem “
\mathrm{posterior}\propto\mathrm{likelihood}\times\mathrm{prior}
”, we can derive a Gaussian posterior for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\textbf{posterior}}

\displaystyle=p(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\propto p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\mu})\times p(\boldsymbol{\mu})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(N\overline{\bm{x}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}N\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)\times\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\bm{m}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}(\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}+N\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}(\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\bm{m}_{0}+N\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\overline{\bm{x}})\right)

\displaystyle\propto\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\bm{m}_{N},\bm{V}_{N})

where
\bm{V}_{N}^{-1}=\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}+N\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}
, and
\bm{m}_{N}=\bm{V}_{N}(\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\bm{m}_{0}+N\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\overline{\bm{x}})
. In which case, the posterior precision matrix is the sum of the prior precision matrix and data precision matrix.
By letting
\bm{V}_{0}\rightarrow\infty\bm{I}
, we can model an uninformative prior such that the posterior distribution of the mean is
p(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\overline{\bm{x}},\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
.

8.5
Posterior distribution of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
: Separated view

Suppose the mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is known in Equation (
), the likelihood is

\displaystyle\mathrm{\textbf{likelihood}}=p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=\prod^{N}_{n=1}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{x}_{n}|\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=(2\pi)^{-ND/2}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\right).

The corresponding conjugate prior is the inverse-Wishart distribution:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\textbf{prior}}=\mathrm{IW}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\bm{S}_{0},\nu_{0})

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+D+1}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\times\left[2^{\frac{\nu_{0}D}{2}}\pi^{D(D-1)/4}|\bm{S}_{0}|^{-\nu_{0}/2}\prod_{d=1}^{D}\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+1-d}{2})\right]^{-1}.

By the Bayes’ theorem “
\mathrm{posterior}\propto\mathrm{likelihood}\times\mathrm{prior}
”, we can derive a inverse-Wishart posterior for
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\textbf{posterior}}

\displaystyle=p(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\mu})\propto p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\times p(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\propto|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\right)\times|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+D+1}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+N+D+1}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}[\bm{S}_{0}+\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}])\right)

\displaystyle\propto\mathrm{IW}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\bm{S}_{0}+\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\nu_{0}+N).

The posterior degree of freedom is the prior degree of freedom
\nu_{0}
plus the number of observations
N
. And the posterior scale matrix is the prior scale matrix
\bm{S}_{0}
plus the data scale matrix
\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.
The mean of the posterior
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu_{0}+N-D-1}(\bm{S}_{0}+\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu_{0}-D-1}{\nu_{0}+N-D-1}\cdot(\frac{1}{\nu_{0}-D-1}\bm{S}_{0})+\frac{N}{\nu_{0}+N-D-1}\cdot(\frac{1}{N}\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot(\frac{1}{\nu_{0}-D-1}\bm{S}_{0})+(1-\lambda)\cdot(\frac{1}{N}\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}),

where
\lambda=\frac{\nu_{0}-D-1}{\nu_{0}+N-D-1}
,
(\frac{1}{\nu_{0}-D-1}\bm{S}_{0})
is the prior mean of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, and
(\frac{1}{N}\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
is an unbiased estimator of the covariance such that
(\frac{1}{N}\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
converges to the true population covariance matrix. Thus, the posterior mean of the covariance matrix can be seen as the weighted average of the prior expectation and the unbiased estimator. The unbiased estimator can also be shown to be equal to the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. As
N\rightarrow\infty
, it can be shown that the posterior expectation of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is a consistent estimator of the population covariance. When we set
\nu_{0}=D+1
,
\lambda=0
and we recover the MLE.

Similarly, the mode of the posterior
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Mode}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu_{0}+N+D+1}(\bm{S}_{0}+\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

(26)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu_{0}+D+1}{\nu_{0}+N+D+1}(\frac{1}{\nu_{0}+D+1}\bm{S}_{0})+\frac{N}{\nu_{0}+N+D+1}(\frac{1}{N}\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\beta(\frac{1}{\nu_{0}+D+1}\bm{S}_{0})+(1-\beta)(\frac{1}{N}\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}),

where
\beta=\frac{\nu_{0}+D+1}{\nu_{0}+N+D+1}
, and
(\frac{1}{\nu_{0}+D+1}\bm{S}_{0})
is the prior mode of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. The posterior mode is a weighted average of the prior mode and the unbiased estimator. Again, the maximum a posterior (MAP) estimator in Equation (
) is a consistent estimator.

8.6
Gibbs sampling of the mean and covariance: Separated view

The separated view here is known as a semi-conjugate prior on the mean and covariance of multivariate Gaussian distribution since both conditionals,
p(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
and
p(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\mu})
, are individually conjugate.
In last two sections, we have shown

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{m}_{N},\bm{V}_{N}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\mu}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{IW}(\bm{S}_{0}+\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\nu_{0}+N).

The two full conditional distributions can be used to construct a Gibbs sampler. The Gibbs sampler generates the mean and covariance
\{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t+1},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t+1}\}
in step
t+1
from
\{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t}\}
in step
t
via the following two steps:

1. Sample
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t+1}
from its full conditional distribution:
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{m}_{N},\bm{V}_{N})
, where
(\bm{m}_{N},\bm{V}_{N})
depend on
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t}
.

2. Sample
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t+1}
from its full conditional distribution:
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t+1}\sim\mathrm{IW}(\bm{S}_{0}+\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\nu_{0}+N)
, where
(\bm{S}_{0}+\bm{S}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\nu_{0}+N)
depend on
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t+1}
.

8.7
Posterior distribution of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
under NIW: Unified view

Likelihood

The likelihood of
N
random observations
\mathcal{X}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
being generated by a multivariate Gaussian with mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is given by Equation (
)

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{ND/2}}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{N}{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\overline{\bm{x}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\overline{\bm{x}})-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{\overline{x}})\right).

Prior

A trivial prior is to combine the conjugate priors for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
respectively in the above sections:

p(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\bm{m}_{0},\bm{V}_{0})\cdot\mathrm{IW}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\bm{S}_{0},\nu_{0}).

However, this is not a conjugate prior to the likelihood with parameters
\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
since
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
appear together in a non-factorized way in the likelihood.
For the full parameters of a multivariate Gaussian distribution (i.e., mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
), the normal-inverse-Wishart (NIW) prior is fully conjugate and defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIW}(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\bm{m}_{0},\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})

(27)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\bm{m}_{0},\frac{1}{\kappa_{0}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\cdot\mathrm{IW}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\bm{S}_{0},\nu_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-1/2}\exp\left(\frac{\kappa_{0}}{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\times|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+D+1}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+D+2}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(-\frac{\kappa_{0}}{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{0})-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{0})\right),

where

Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})=2^{\frac{(\nu_{0}+1)D}{2}}\pi^{D(D+1)/4}\kappa_{0}^{-D/2}|\bm{S}_{0}|^{-\nu_{0}/2}\prod_{d=1}^{D}\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+1-d}{2}).

(28)

The specific form of the normalization term
Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})
will be useful to show the posterior marginal likelihood of the data in Section
.

A “prior” interpretation for the NIW prior

The inverse-Wishart distribution will ensure that the resulting covariance matrix is positive definite when
\nu_{0}>D
. And if we are confident that the true covariance matrix is near some covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
, then we might choose
\nu_{0}
to be large and set
\bm{S}_{0}=(\nu_{0}-D-1)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
, making the distribution of the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
concentrated around
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
. On the other hand, choosing
\nu_{0}=D+2
and
\bm{S}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
will make
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
loosely concentrated around
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
.
More details can be referred to
(
Chipman et al. 2001
;
Fraley and Raftery 2007
;
Hoff 2009
;
Murphy 2012
)
.

An intuitive interpretation of the hyper-parameters
(
Murphy 2012
;
Hoff 2009
)
:
\bm{m}_{0}
is our prior
mean for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
\kappa_{0}
is how strongly we believe this prior for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
(the larger the stronger we believe this prior mean),
\bm{S}_{0}
is proportional to our prior mean for
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, and
\nu_{0}
controls how strongly we believe this prior for
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. Because the Gamma function is not defined for negative integers and zero, from Equation (
) we require
\nu_{0}>D-1
(which also can be shown from the expectation of the covariance matrix Equation (
). And also
\bm{S}_{0}
needs to be a positive definite matrix, where an intuitive reason can be shown from Equation (
). A more detailed reason can be found in
(
Hoff 2009
)
.

Posterior

By the Bayes’ theorem “
\mathrm{posterior}\propto\mathrm{likelihood}\times\mathrm{prior}
”, the posterior of the
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
parameters under the NIW prior is

p(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})\propto p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})p(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})=p(\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta}),

(29)

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\bm{m}_{0},\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})
are the hyperparameters and the right hand side of Equation (
) is also known as the full joint distribution
p(\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})
, and is given by

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\cdot p(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})

(30)

\displaystyle=C\times|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+N+D+2}{2}}\times

\displaystyle\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{N}{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\overline{\bm{x}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\overline{\bm{x}})-\frac{\kappa_{0}}{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{0})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{\overline{x}})-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{0})\Bigg\},

where
C=\frac{(2\pi)^{-ND/2}}{Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})}
is a constant normalization term.
This can be reduced to

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})

(31)

\displaystyle=C|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+N+D+2}{2}}\times

\displaystyle\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{\kappa_{0}+N}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\frac{\kappa_{0}\bm{m}_{0}+N\overline{\bm{x}}}{\kappa_{N}}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\frac{\kappa_{0}\bm{m}_{0}+N\overline{\bm{x}}}{\kappa_{N}}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\left(\bm{S}_{0}+\bm{S}_{\overline{x}}+\frac{\kappa_{0}N}{\kappa_{0}+N}(\overline{\bm{x}}-\bm{m}_{0})(\overline{\bm{x}}-\bm{m}_{0})^{\top}\right)\right]\Bigg\},

which is calculated to compare with the NIW form in Equation (
), and we can see the reason why we rewrite the multivariate Gaussian distribution into Equation (
19
) by the trace trick. It follows that the posterior is also a NIW density with updated parameters and gives the view of conjugacy for multivariate Gaussian distribution:

p(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})=\mathrm{NIW}(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\bm{m}_{N},\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},\bm{S}_{N}),

(32)

where

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{0}\bm{m}_{0}+N\overline{\bm{x}}}{\kappa_{N}}=\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{N}}\bm{m}_{0}+\frac{N}{\kappa_{N}}\overline{\bm{x}}

(33)

\displaystyle\kappa_{N}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}+N

(34)

\displaystyle\nu_{N}

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}+N

(35)

\displaystyle\bm{S}_{N}

\displaystyle=\bm{S}_{0}+\bm{S}_{\overline{x}}+\frac{\kappa_{0}N}{\kappa_{0}+N}(\overline{\bm{x}}-\bm{m}_{0})(\overline{\bm{x}}-\bm{m}_{0})^{\top}

(36)

\displaystyle=\bm{S}_{0}+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bm{x}_{n}\bm{x}_{n}^{\top}+\kappa_{0}\bm{m}_{0}\bm{m}_{0}^{\top}-\kappa_{N}\bm{m}_{N}\bm{m}_{N}^{\top}.

(37)

A “posterior” interpretation for the NIW prior

An intuitive interpretation for the parameters in NIW can be obtained from the updated parameters above.
\nu_{0}
is the prior number of samples to observe the covariance matrix, and
\nu_{N}=\nu_{0}+N
is the posterior number of samples. The posterior mean
\bm{m}_{N}
of the model mean
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is a weighted average of the prior mean and the sample mean. The posterior scale matrix
\bm{S}_{N}
is the sum of the prior scale matrix, empirical covariance matrix
\bm{S}_{\overline{x}}
, and an extra term due to the uncertainty in the mean.

8.7.1
Parameter choice

In practice, it is often better to use a weakly informative data-dependent prior. A common choice is to set
\bm{S}_{0}=\mathrm{diag}(\bm{S}_{\overline{x}})/N
, and
\nu_{0}=D+2
, to ensure
\mathrm{E}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}]=\bm{S}_{0}
, and to set
\bm{m}_{0}=\overline{\bm{x}}
and
\kappa_{0}
to some small number, such as 0.01, where
\bm{S}_{\overline{x}}
is the sample covariance matrix and
\overline{\bm{x}}
is the sample mean vector as shown in Equation (
)
(
Chipman et al. 2001
;
Fraley and Raftery 2007
;
Hoff 2009
;
Murphy 2012
)
. Equivalently, we can also standardize the observation matrix
\mathcal{X}
first to have zero mean and unit variance for every feature, and then let
\bm{S}_{0}=\bm{I}_{D}
, and
\nu_{0}=D+2
, to ensure
\mathrm{E}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}]=\bm{I}_{D}
, and to set
\bm{m}_{0}=\bm{0}_{D}
and
\kappa_{0}
to some small number, such as 0.01.

8.7.2
Reducing sampling time by maintaining squared sum of customers

In this section, we introduce some tricks to implement NIW in Gaussian mixture model more efficiently. The content can also be found in
(
Das 2014
)
. The readers will better understand the Chinese restaurant process terminology in this section after reading Section
or Section
. Feel free to skip this section on a first reading.

We have seen the equivalence between the Equation (
36
) and Equation (
37
). The reason why we make a step further to Equation (
37
) from Equation (
36
) is to reduce sampling time.
Suppose now that the data is not fixed and some data points can be removed from or added to
\mathcal{X}
. If we stick to the form in Equation (
36
), we need to calculate
\bm{S}_{\overline{x}}
and
\overline{\bm{x}}
over and over again whenever the data points are updated.

In Chinese restaurant process/clustering terminology, if we use Equation (
36
) instead of Equation (
37
), whenever a customer is removed from (or added to) a table, we have to compute the matrix
\bm{S}_{\overline{x}}
, which requires to go over each point in this cluster (or each customer in this table following the term from Chinese restaurant process, this could be clear when you finish reading the collapsed Gibbs sampler for finite Gaussian mixture model or infinite Gaussian mixture model later). Computing this term everytime when a customer is removed or added, could be computationally expensive.

We realize that the data terms in Equation (
37
) only involves a sum of the outer product which does not contain any cross product (e.g.,
\bm{x}_{i}\bm{x}_{j}^{\top}
for
i\neq j
).
By reformulating into Equation (
37
), whenever a customer is removed or added, we just have to subtract or add
\bm{x}_{n}\bm{x}_{n}^{\top}
. Thus for each table, we only have to maintain the squared sum of customer vectors
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bm{x}_{n}\bm{x}_{n}^{\top}
for
\bm{S}_{N}
.

Similarly, for
\bm{m}_{N}
, we need to maintain the sum of customer vectors
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bm{x}_{n}
for the same reason from Equation (
33
).

8.8
Posterior marginal likelihood of parameters

The posterior marginal for
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is given by

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}p(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})d\boldsymbol{\mu}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{IW}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\bm{S}_{N},\nu_{N}),

where the mean and mode can be obtained by Equation
, and they are given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{S}_{N}}{\nu_{N}-D-1},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Mode}[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{S}_{N}}{\nu_{N}+D+1}.

The posterior marginal for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
follows from a Student
t
distribution. We can show the posterior marginal for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is given by

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}p(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})d\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\int_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\mathrm{NIW}(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\bm{m}_{N},\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},\bm{S}_{N})d\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\tau(\boldsymbol{\mu}|\bm{m}_{N},\frac{1}{\kappa_{N}(\nu_{N}-D+1)}\bm{S}_{N},\nu_{N}-D+1),

which is from the Gaussian scale mixture property of Student
t
distribution, see Equation
and further discussed in
(
Murphy 2012
)
.

8.9
Posterior marginal likelihood of data

By integrating the full joint distribution in Equation (
), we can get the marginal likelihood of data under hyper-parameter
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\bm{m}_{0},\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})
:

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}p(\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})d\boldsymbol{\mu}d\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

(38)

\displaystyle=\int_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\cdot\mathrm{NIW}(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})d\boldsymbol{\mu}d\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{-ND/2}}{Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})}\int_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+N+D+2}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(-\frac{\kappa_{N}}{2}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{N})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bm{m}_{N})-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_{N})\right)d\boldsymbol{\mu}d\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{=}(2\pi)^{-ND/2}\frac{Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},\bm{S}_{N})}{Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})}

\displaystyle=\pi^{-\frac{ND}{2}}\cdot\frac{\kappa_{0}^{D/2}\cdot|\bm{S}_{0}|^{\nu_{0}/2}}{\kappa_{N}^{D/2}\cdot|\bm{S}_{N}|^{\nu_{N}/2}}\prod_{d=1}^{D}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N}+1-d}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+1-d}{2})},

where the Identity (*) above is from the fact that the integral reduces to the normalizing constant of the NIW density given in Equation (
).

8.10
Posterior predictive for data without observations

Similarly, suppose now we observe a data vector
\bm{x}^{\star}
without observing any old datas. Then the predictive for the data vector can be obtained by

\displaystyle p(\bm{x}^{\star}|\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}p(\bm{x}^{\star},\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})d\boldsymbol{\mu}d\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

(39)

\displaystyle=\int_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}^{\star}|\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\cdot\mathrm{NIW}(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})d\boldsymbol{\mu}d\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\pi^{-D/2}\frac{\kappa_{0}^{D/2}|\bm{S}_{0}|^{\nu_{0}/2}}{(\kappa_{0}+1)^{D/2}|\bm{S}_{1}|^{\nu_{1}/2}}\prod_{d=1}^{D}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{1}+1-d}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+1-d}{2})}

\displaystyle=\pi^{-D/2}\frac{\kappa_{0}^{D/2}|\bm{S}_{0}|^{\nu_{0}/2}}{(\kappa_{0}+1)^{D/2}|\bm{S}_{1}|^{\nu_{1}/2}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+2-D}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}}{2})},

where
\nu_{1}=\nu_{0}+1
,
\bm{S}_{1}=\bm{S}_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{0}+1}(\bm{x}^{\star}-\bm{m}_{0})(\bm{x}^{\star}-\bm{m}_{0})^{\top}
.
An alternative form of Equation (
) is to rewrite by a multivariate Student
t
distribution

p(\bm{x}^{\star}|\boldsymbol{\beta})=\tau(\bm{x}^{\star}|\bm{m}_{0},\frac{\kappa_{0}+1}{\kappa_{0}(\nu_{0}-D+1)}\bm{S}_{0},\nu_{0}-D+1).

(40)

8.11
Posterior predictive for new data with observations

Similar to posterior predictive for data without observation, now suppose we observe a new data vector
\bm{x}^{\star}
give old observations
\mathcal{X}
. Then the posterior predictive for this vector is

p(\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})=\frac{p(\bm{x}^{\star},\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\beta})}{p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\beta})}.

(41)

The denominator of Equation (
) can be obtained directly from Equation (
). The numerator of it can be obtained in a similar way from Equation (
) by considering the marginal likelihood of the new set
\{\mathcal{X},\bm{x}^{\star}\}
. We just need to replace
N
by
N^{\star}=N+1
in Equation (
33
), Equation (
34
), and Equation (
35
), and replace
\bm{S}_{N}
by
\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}
in Equation (
36
). Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle p(\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-D/2}\frac{Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{N^{\star}},\nu_{N^{\star}},\bm{S}_{N^{\star}})}{Z_{\mathrm{NIW}}(D,\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},\bm{S}_{N})}

(42)

\displaystyle=\pi^{-D/2}\frac{(\kappa_{N^{\star}})^{-D/2}|\bm{S}_{N}|^{(\nu_{N})/2}}{(\kappa_{N})^{-D/2}|\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}|^{(\nu_{N^{\star}})/2}}\prod_{d=1}^{D}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N^{\star}}+1-d}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N}+1-d}{2})}

\displaystyle=\pi^{-D/2}\frac{(\kappa_{N^{\star}})^{-D/2}|\bm{S}_{N}|^{(\nu_{N})/2}}{(\kappa_{N})^{-D/2}|\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}|^{(\nu_{N^{\star}})/2}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+N+2-D}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+N}{2})}.

Again an alternative form of Equation (
) is to rewrite by a multivariate Student
t
distribution:

p(\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})=\tau(\bm{x}^{\star}|\bm{m}_{N},\frac{\kappa_{N}+1}{\kappa_{N}(\nu_{N}-D+1)}\bm{S}_{N},\nu_{N}-D+1).

Thus, the mean and covariance of
\bm{x}^{\star}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}]

\displaystyle=\bm{m}_{N}=\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{0}+N}\bm{m}_{0}+\frac{N}{\kappa_{0}+N}\overline{\bm{x}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{N}+1}{\kappa_{N}(\nu_{N}-D-1)}\bm{S}_{N}=\frac{\kappa_{0}+N+1}{(\kappa_{0}+N)(\nu_{0}+N-D-1)}\bm{S}_{N},

where we can find, on average, the new coming data has expectation
\bm{m}_{N}
. We mentioned previously,
\kappa_{0}
controls how strongly we believe this prior for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
. When
\kappa_{0}
is large enough,
\mathrm{E}[\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}]
converges to
\bm{m}_{0}
, the prior mean, and
\mathrm{Cov}[\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}]
converges to
\frac{\bm{S}_{N}}{(\kappa_{0}+N)(\nu_{0}+N-D-1)}
. In the meantime, if we set
\nu_{0}
large enough, the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
concentrated around
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
, and

\bm{S}_{N}\rightarrow\frac{\bm{S}_{\overline{x}}}{\nu_{0}}+\frac{\kappa_{0}N}{\nu_{0}(\kappa_{0}+N)}(\overline{\bm{x}}-\bm{m}_{0})(\overline{\bm{x}}-\bm{m}_{0})^{\top},

which is largely controlled by data sample and data magnitude (rather than the prior hyperparameters), so as the posterior variance
\mathrm{Cov}[\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}]
.

8.12
Further optimization via the Cholesky decomposition

8.12.1
Definition

The Cholesky decomposition of a symmetric positive definite matrix
\bm{S}
is its decomposition into the product of a lower triangular matrix
\bm{L}
and it’s transpose:

\bm{S}=\bm{L}\bm{L}^{\top},

(43)

where
\bm{L}
is called the
Cholesky factor
of
\bm{S}
. We realize that an alternative form of the Cholesky decomposition is using it’s upper triangular
\bm{U}
, i.e.,
\bm{S}=\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{U}
. A triangular matrix is a special kind of square matrix. Specifically, a square matrix is called lower triangular if all the entries are above the main diagonal are zero. Similarly, a square matrix is called upper triangular if all the entries below the main diagonal are zero.

If the matrix has dimensionality
D
, the complexity of Cholesky decomposition is
O(D^{3})
. In specific, it requires
\sim\frac{1}{3}D^{3}
floating points operations (flops) to compute a Cholesky decomposition
of a
D\times D
positive definite matrix
(
Lu 2021a
)
, where the symbol “
\sim
” has the usual asymptotic meaning

\lim_{D\to+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{number\,of\,flops}}{(1/3)D^{3}}=1.

The existence of Cholesky decomposition is based on the existence of the LU decomposition and a rigorous proof can be found in
(
Lu 2021a
)
.

8.12.2
Rank one update

A rank 1 update of matrix
\bm{S}
by vector
\bm{x}
is of the form
(
Seeger 2004
)

\bm{S}^{\prime}=\bm{S}+\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\top}.

If we have already calculated the Cholesky factor
\bm{L}
of
\bm{S}
, then the Cholesky factor
\bm{L}^{\prime}
of
\bm{S}^{\prime}
can be calculated efficiently. Note that
\bm{S}^{\prime}
differs from
\bm{S}
only via three symmetric rank one matrices. Hence we can compute
\bm{L}^{\prime}
from
\bm{L}
using three rank one Cholesky update, which takes
O(D^{2})
operations each saving from
O(D^{3})
if we do know
\bm{L}
, the Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{S}
.

8.12.3
Speedup for determinant

The determinant of a positive definite matrix
\bm{S}
can be computed from its Cholesky factor
\bm{L}
:

|\bm{S}|=\prod_{d=1}^{D}\bm{L}_{dd}^{2},\qquad\log(|\bm{S}|)=2\log(|\bm{L}|)=2\times\sum_{d=1}^{D}\log(\bm{L}_{dd}),

where
\bm{L}_{dd}
is the (
d,d
) entry of matrix
\bm{L}
. This is an
O(D)
operation, i.e., given the Cholesky decomposition, the determinant is just the product of the diagonal terms.

8.12.4
Update in NIW

Now we consider computing the marginal likelihood of data in Equation (
) and the posterior predictive for new coming data in Equation (
) of which the two cases are similar. We will see this optimization will be often used in the Chinese restaurant process like that in Section
. Feel free to skip this section on a first reading.

Take the latter as an example, note that to compute posterior predictive for new coming data
p(\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})
in Equation (
), we just need to evaluate
\frac{p(\bm{x}^{\star},\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\beta})}{p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\beta})}
, in which we must calculate
|\bm{S}_{N}|
and
|\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}|
efficiently where
N^{\star}=N+1
.We deal with computing the determinants
|\bm{S}_{N}|
and
|\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}|
by representing
\bm{S}_{N}
and
\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}
using their Cholesky decomposition. In particular, updates to
\bm{S}_{N}
and
\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}
will be carried out by directly updating their Cholesky decompositions given the Cholesky decomposition the determinant is just the product of the diagonal terms. Write out
\bm{S}_{\star}
by
\bm{S}_{N}
:

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{N^{\star}}\bm{m}_{N^{\star}}-x^{\star}}{\kappa_{N}}=\frac{(\kappa_{0}+N+1)\bm{m}_{N^{\star}}-x^{\star}}{\kappa_{0}+N},

(44)

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{N^{\star}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{N}\bm{m}_{N}+\bm{x}^{\star}}{\kappa_{N^{\star}}}=\frac{(\kappa_{0}+N)\bm{m}_{N}+\bm{x}^{\star}}{\kappa_{0}+N+1},

(45)

\displaystyle\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}

\displaystyle=\bm{S}_{N}+\bm{x}^{\star}\bm{x}^{\star T}-\kappa_{N^{\star}}\bm{m}_{N^{\star}}\bm{m}_{N^{\star}}^{\top}+\kappa_{N}\bm{m}_{N}\bm{m}_{N}^{\top}

(46)

\displaystyle=\bm{S}_{N}+\frac{\kappa_{0}+N+1}{\kappa_{0}+N}(\bm{m}_{N^{\star}}-\bm{x}^{\star})(\bm{m}_{N^{\star}}-\bm{x}^{\star})^{\top},

(47)

where Equation (
47
) implies that Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}
can be obtained from Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{S}_{N}
by a Rank 1 update. Therefore if we know the Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{S}_{N}
, the Cholesky decomposition of
\bm{S}_{N^{\star}}
can be obtained in
O(D^{2})
complexity.

8.13
Last words on the conjugate prior for Gaussian distribution

The univariate analog of normal-inverse-Wishart distribution is the mormal-inverse-Chi-squared (NIX) distribution.
For simplicity, we only write the likelihood and prior distribution for univariate Gaussian distribution here, all the analysis in the following sections will be described in the multivariate case.

Likelihood

The univariate Gaussian distribution is

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{X}|\mu,\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=\prod^{N}_{n=1}\mathcal{N}(x_{n}|\mu,\sigma^{2})

(48)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-N/2}(\sigma^{2})^{-N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[N(\overline{x}-\mu)^{2}+N\sum_{n=1}^{N}(x_{n}-\overline{x})^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-N/2}(\sigma^{2})^{-N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[N(\overline{x}-\mu)^{2}+NS_{\overline{x}}\right]\right),

where
S_{\overline{x}}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}(x_{n}-\overline{x})^{2}
.

8.13.1
Normal-inverse-chi-squared prior

Prior on parameters

Follow from the definition of inverse-gamma distribution in Definition
, we give the rigorous definition of inverse-chi-squared distribution as follows.

Definition 15

(Inverse-Chi-Squared Distribution)
.

A random variable
Y
is said to follow the inverse-chi-squared distribution with parameter
\nu_{0}>0
and
\sigma_{0}^{2}>0
if
Y\sim\mathrm{IG}(\frac{\nu_{0}}{2},\frac{\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2})
:

f(y;\nu_{0},\sigma_{0}^{2})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{{(\frac{\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2})}^{\frac{\nu_{0}}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}}{2})}y^{-\frac{\nu_{0}}{2}-1}\exp(-\frac{\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2y}),&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}y>0.\\
&0,&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}y\leq 0.\end{aligned}\right.

And it is denoted by
Y\sim\mathrm{\chi^{-2}}(\nu_{0},\sigma_{0}^{2})
. The parameter
\nu>0
is called the
degrees of freedom
, and
\sigma_{0}^{2}>0
is the
scale parameter
. And it is also known as the
scaled
inverse-chi-squared distribution.
The mean and variance of inverse-gamma distribution are given by

\mathrm{E}[Y]=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{\nu_{0}-2},\,&\mathrm{if\,}\nu_{0}\geq 2.\\
&\infty,\,&\mathrm{if\,}0<\nu_{0}<2.\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\mathrm{Var}[Y]=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{2\nu_{0}^{2}\sigma_{0}^{4}}{(\nu_{0}-2)^{2}(\nu_{0}-4)},\,&\mathrm{if\,}\nu_{0}\geq 4.\\
&\infty,\,&\mathrm{if\,}0<\nu_{0}<4.\end{aligned}\right.

To make connection to inverse-Wishart distribution, we can set
S_{0}=\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}
. Then the inverse-Chi-squared distribution can also be denoted by
Y\sim\mathrm{IG}(\frac{\nu_{0}}{2},\frac{S_{0}}{2})
if
Y\sim\mathrm{\chi^{-2}}(\nu_{0},\sigma_{0}^{2})
of which the form conforms to the univariate case of inverse-Wishart distribution. And we will see the similarity in the posterior parameters as well.

Similarly to the normal-inverse-Wishart prior, the normal-inverse-chi-squared prior is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIX}(\mu,\sigma^{2}|m_{0},\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},S_{0})

(49)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\mu|m_{0},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\kappa_{0}})\cdot\mathrm{\chi^{-2}}(\sigma^{2}|\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})}(\sigma^{2})^{-(\nu_{0}/2+3/2)}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[\nu_{0}\sigma^{2}_{0}+\kappa_{0}(m_{0}-\mu)^{2}\right]\right),

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{0}=\nu_{0}\sigma^{2}_{0}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})}(\sigma^{2})^{-(\nu_{0}/2+3/2)}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[S_{0}+\kappa_{0}(m_{0}-\mu)^{2}\right]\right)

where

Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})=\frac{\sqrt{(2\pi)}}{\sqrt{\kappa_{0}}}\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}}{2})(\frac{2}{\nu_{0}\sigma^{2}_{0}})^{\nu_{0}/2}=\frac{\sqrt{(2\pi)}}{\sqrt{\kappa_{0}}}\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}}{2})(\frac{2}{S_{0}})^{\nu_{0}/2}.

(50)

Posterior under NIX

Again, by the Bayes’ theorem “
\mathrm{posterior}\propto\mathrm{likelihood}\times\mathrm{prior}
”, the posterior of the
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
parameters under the NIW prior is

\displaystyle p(\mu,\sigma^{2}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle\propto p(\mathcal{X}|\mu,\sigma^{2})p(\mu,\sigma^{2}|\boldsymbol{\beta})=p(\mathcal{X},\mu,\sigma^{2}|\boldsymbol{\beta})

(51)

\displaystyle=C\times(\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+3+N}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[N(\overline{x}-\mu)^{2}+NS_{\overline{x}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[S_{0}+\kappa_{0}(m_{0}-\mu)^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=C\times(\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+3}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[S_{N}+\kappa_{N}(m_{N}-\mu)^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\propto\mathrm{NIX}(\mu,\sigma^{2}|m_{N},\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S_{N}})=\mathcal{N}(\mu|m_{N},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\kappa_{N}})\cdot\mathrm{\chi^{-2}}(\sigma^{2}|\nu_{N},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma^{2}_{N}}),

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(m_{0},\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},S_{0}=\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2})
,
C=\frac{(2\pi)^{-N/2}}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})}
, and

\displaystyle m_{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{0}m_{0}+N\overline{x}}{\kappa_{N}}=\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{N}}m_{0}+\frac{N}{\kappa_{N}}\overline{x},

\displaystyle\kappa_{N}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}+N,

\displaystyle\nu_{N}

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}+N,

\displaystyle S_{N}

\displaystyle=S_{0}+NS_{\overline{x}}+N\overline{x}^{2}+\kappa_{0}m_{0}^{2}-\kappa_{N}m_{N}^{2}

\displaystyle=S_{0}+NS_{\overline{x}}+\frac{\kappa_{0}N}{\kappa_{0}+N}(\overline{x}-m_{0})^{2},

\displaystyle\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2}

\displaystyle=S_{N}\qquad\underrightarrow{\text{leads to}}\qquad\sigma_{N}^{2}=\frac{S_{N}}{\nu_{N}},

which shares same form as that in the multivariate case from Equation
except the
N
in
NS_{\overline{x}}
which arise from the difference between the multivariate Gaussian distribution and the univariate Gaussian distribution. Similarly, in inverse-chi-squared language, we can show the
\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2}=S_{N}
.

Suppose
\nu_{0}\geq 2
, or
N\geq 2
such that
\nu_{N}\geq 2
, the posterior expectations are given by

\mathrm{E}[\mu|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}]=m_{N},\qquad\mathrm{E}[\sigma^{2}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta}]=\frac{S_{N}}{\nu_{N}-2}.

Marginal posterior of
\sigma^{2}

Integrate out
\mu
, we have

\displaystyle p(\sigma^{2}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mu}p(\mu,\sigma^{2}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mu}\mathcal{N}(\mu|m_{N},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\kappa_{N}})\cdot\mathrm{\chi^{-2}}(\sigma^{2}|\nu_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N})d\mu

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\chi^{-2}}(\sigma^{2}|\nu_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N}),

which is just an integral over a Gaussian distribution.

Marginal posterior of
\mu

Integrate out
\sigma^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle p(\mu|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\sigma^{2}}p(\mu,\sigma^{2}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})d\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{N}(\mu|m_{N},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\kappa_{N}})\cdot\mathrm{\chi^{-2}}(\sigma^{2}|\nu_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N})d\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\sigma^{2}}C(\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+3}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[S_{N}+\kappa_{N}(m_{N}-\mu)^{2}\right]\right)d\sigma^{2}.

Let
\phi=\sigma^{2}
and
\alpha=(\nu_{N}+1)/2
,
A=S_{N}+\kappa_{N}(m_{N}-\mu)^{2}
, and
x=\frac{A}{2\phi}
, we have

\frac{d\phi}{dx}=-\frac{A}{2}x^{-2}.

where
A
can be easily verified to be positive and
\phi=\sigma^{2}>0
.
It follows that

\displaystyle p(\mu|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}C(\phi)^{-\alpha-1}\exp\left(-\frac{A}{2\phi}\right)d\phi

\displaystyle=\int_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\infty}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}C(\frac{A}{2x})^{-\alpha-1}\exp\left(-x\right)({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-}\frac{A}{2}x^{-2})dx\qquad

\displaystyle\text{(since $x=\frac{A}{2\phi}$)}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\infty}}C(\frac{A}{2x})^{-\alpha-1}\exp\left(-x\right)(\frac{A}{2}x^{-2})dx

\displaystyle=(\frac{A}{2})^{-\alpha}\int_{x}Cx^{\alpha-1}\exp\left(-x\right)dx

\displaystyle=(\frac{A}{2})^{-\alpha}(C\cdot\Gamma(1))\int_{x}\mathrm{Ga}(x|\alpha,1)dx\qquad

\displaystyle\text{(see Definition\penalty\ \ref{definition:gamma-distribution})}

\displaystyle=(C\cdot\Gamma(1))\left[\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2}+\kappa_{N}(m_{N}-\mu)^{2}\right]^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}(C\cdot\Gamma(1))(\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2})^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2}}\left[1+\frac{\kappa_{N}}{\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2}}(m_{N}-\mu)^{2}\right]^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2}}

We notice that
C
is defined in Equation
(in terms of
(\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N})
) that

C\overset{(b)}{=}\frac{(2\pi)^{-N/2}}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N})}=\frac{(2\pi)^{-N/2}}{\frac{\sqrt{(2\pi)}}{\sqrt{\kappa_{N}}}\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N}}{2})(\frac{2}{\nu_{N}\sigma^{2}_{N}})^{\nu_{N}/2}}\propto(\nu_{N}\sigma^{2}_{N})^{\nu_{N}/2}.

Combine Equation (a) and (b) above, we obtain

p(\mu|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})\propto\frac{1}{\sigma_{N}/\sqrt{\kappa_{N}}}\left[1+\frac{\kappa_{N}}{\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2}}(\mu-m_{N})^{2}\right]^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2}}\propto\tau(\mu|m_{N},\sigma_{N}^{2}/\kappa_{N},\nu_{N}),

which is a univariate Student
t
distribution see Definition
.

Marginal likelihood of data

By Equation
, we can get the marginal likelihood of data under hyper-parameter
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(m_{0},\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},S_{0}=\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2})

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mu}\int_{\sigma^{2}}p(\mathcal{X},\mu,\sigma^{2}|\boldsymbol{\beta})d\mu d\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{-N/2}}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})}\int_{\mu}\int_{\sigma^{2}}(\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+3}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[S_{N}+\kappa_{N}(m_{N}-\mu)^{2}\right]\right)d\mu d\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-N/2}\frac{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N})}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})}

\displaystyle=(\pi)^{-N/2}\frac{\Gamma(\nu_{N}/2)}{\Gamma(\nu_{0}/2)}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{N}}}\frac{(\nu_{0}\sigma^{2}_{0})^{\nu_{0}/2}}{(\nu_{N}\sigma^{2}_{N})^{\nu_{N}/2}}.

Posterior predictive for new data with observations

Let the number of samples for data set
\{x^{\star},\mathcal{X}\}
be
{N^{\star}}=N+1
, we have

\displaystyle p(x^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{p(x^{\star},\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\beta})}{p(\mathcal{X}|\boldsymbol{\beta})}

(52)

\displaystyle=\left\{(2\pi)^{-{N^{\star}}/2}\frac{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{N^{\star}},\nu_{N^{\star}},\sigma^{2}_{N^{\star}})}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})}\right\}/\left\{(2\pi)^{-N/2}\frac{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N})}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})}\right\}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\frac{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{N^{\star}},\nu_{N^{\star}},\sigma^{2}_{N^{\star}})}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{N},\nu_{N},\sigma^{2}_{N})}

\displaystyle=(\pi)^{-1/2}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa_{N}}{\kappa_{N^{\star}}}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N^{\star}}}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N}}{2})}\frac{(\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2})^{\frac{\nu_{N}}{2}}}{(\nu_{{N^{\star}}}\sigma_{{N^{\star}}}^{2})^{\frac{\nu_{{N^{\star}}}}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N}}{2})}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa_{N}}{(\kappa_{N}+1)}\frac{1}{(\pi\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2})}}\left(\frac{(\nu_{{N^{\star}}}\sigma_{{N^{\star}}}^{2})}{(\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2})}\right)^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2}}.

We realize that

\displaystyle m_{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{N^{\star}}m_{N^{\star}}-x^{\star}}{\kappa_{N}}=\frac{(\kappa_{0}+N+1)m_{N^{\star}}-x^{\star}}{\kappa_{0}+N},

\displaystyle m_{N^{\star}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{N}m_{N}+x^{\star}}{\kappa_{N^{\star}}}=\frac{(\kappa_{0}+N)m_{N}+x^{\star}}{\kappa_{0}+N+1},

\displaystyle S_{N^{\star}}

\displaystyle=S_{N}+x^{\star}x^{\star T}-\kappa_{N^{\star}}m_{N^{\star}}^{2}+\kappa_{N}m_{N}^{2}

\displaystyle=S_{N}+\frac{\kappa_{N}+1}{\kappa_{N}}(m_{N^{\star}}-x^{\star})^{2}

\displaystyle=S_{N}+\frac{\kappa_{N}}{\kappa_{N}+1}(m_{N}-x^{\star})^{2},

Thus,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{(\nu_{{N^{\star}}}\sigma_{{N^{\star}}}^{2})}{(\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2})}\right)^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{S_{N^{\star}}}{S_{N}}\right)^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2}}=1+\frac{\kappa_{N}(m_{N}-x^{\star})^{2}}{(\kappa_{N}+1)\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2}}.

(53)

Substitute Equation
into Equation
, it follows that

\displaystyle p(\bm{x}^{\star}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{N}}{2})}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa_{N}}{(\kappa_{N}+1)}\frac{1}{(\pi\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2})}}\left(1+\frac{\kappa_{N}(m_{N}-x^{\star})^{2}}{(\kappa_{N}+1)\nu_{N}\sigma_{N}^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{\nu_{N}+1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\tau(x^{\star}|m_{N},\frac{\kappa_{N}+1}{\kappa_{N}}\sigma^{2}_{N},\nu_{N}).

Posterior predictive for new data without observations

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle p(x^{\star}|\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mu}\int_{\sigma^{2}}p(x^{\star},\mu,\sigma^{2}|\boldsymbol{\beta})d\mu d\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\frac{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{1},\nu_{1},\sigma^{2}_{1})}{Z_{\mathrm{NIX}}(\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\sigma^{2}_{0})}

\displaystyle=(\pi)^{-1/2}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{1}}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{1}}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}}{2})}\frac{(\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2})^{\frac{\nu_{0}}{2}}}{(\nu_{1}\sigma_{1}^{2})^{\frac{\nu_{1}}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}+1}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\nu_{0}}{2})}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa_{0}}{(\kappa_{0}+1)}\frac{1}{(\pi\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2})}}\left(\frac{(\nu_{1}\sigma_{1}^{2})}{(\nu_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2})}\right)^{-\frac{\nu_{0}+1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\tau(x^{\star}|m_{0},\frac{\kappa_{0}+1}{\kappa_{0}}\sigma^{2}_{0},\nu_{0}).

8.13.2
Normal-inverse-gamma prior*

Prior on parameters

We realize that inverse-chi-squared distribution is a special inverse-gamma distribution (defined in Definition
). The particularity is in the similarity with the inverse-Wishart distribution. Similarly and more generally, we can define the normal-inverse-gamma prior as follows (as we have shown the inverse-gamma distribution is often used as a conjugate prior for the variance parameter in Section
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIG}(\mu,\sigma^{2}|m_{0},\kappa_{0},r_{0},\lambda_{0})

(54)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\mu|m_{0},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\kappa_{0}})\cdot\mathrm{IG}(\sigma^{2}|r_{0},\lambda_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathrm{NIG}}(\kappa_{0},r_{0},\lambda_{0})}(\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2r_{0}+3}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[\kappa_{0}(m_{0}-\mu)^{2}+2\lambda_{0}\right]\right)

where

Z_{\mathrm{NIG}}(\kappa_{0},r_{0},\lambda_{0})=\frac{\Gamma(r_{0})}{\lambda_{0}^{r_{0}}}(2\pi)^{-1/2}.

(55)

This is equivalent to set
r_{0}=\frac{\nu_{0}}{2}
and
\lambda_{0}=\frac{S_{0}}{2}
in
\mathrm{NIX}
.

Posterior under NIG

Again, by the Bayes’ theorem “
\mathrm{posterior}\propto\mathrm{likelihood}\times\mathrm{prior}
”, the posterior of the
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
parameters under the NIG prior is

\displaystyle p(\mu,\sigma^{2}|\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle\propto p(\mathcal{X}|\mu,\sigma^{2})p(\mu,\sigma^{2}|\boldsymbol{\beta})=p(\mathcal{X},\mu,\sigma^{2}|\boldsymbol{\beta})

(56)

\displaystyle=C\times(\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2r_{0}+3+N}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[N(\overline{x}-\mu)^{2}+NS_{\overline{x}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[2\lambda_{0}+\kappa_{0}(m_{0}-\mu)^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\propto(\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2r_{N}+3}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[\lambda_{N}+\kappa_{N}(m_{N}-\mu)^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\propto\mathrm{NIG}(\mu,\sigma^{2}|m_{N},\kappa_{N},r_{N},\lambda_{N}).

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(m_{0},\kappa_{0},r_{0},\lambda_{0})
,
C=\frac{(2\pi)^{-N/2}}{Z_{\mathrm{NIG}}(\kappa_{0},r_{0},\lambda_{0})}
, and

\displaystyle m_{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{0}m_{0}+N\overline{x}}{\kappa_{N}}=\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\kappa_{N}}m_{0}+\frac{N}{\kappa_{N}}\overline{x},

\displaystyle\kappa_{N}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}+N,

\displaystyle r_{N}

\displaystyle=r_{0}+\frac{N}{2},

\displaystyle\lambda_{N}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(NS_{\overline{x}}+N\overline{x}^{2}+\kappa_{0}m_{0}^{2}-\kappa_{N}m_{N}^{2})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(NS_{\overline{x}}+\frac{\kappa_{0}N}{\kappa_{0}+N}(\overline{x}-m_{0})^{2}).

Further discussion on the posterior marginal likelihood can be found in
(
Murphy 2007
)
. We will leave this to the readers as it is rather similar as that in the NIX prior.

Part IV
Bayesian inference for mixture model

9
General mixture model

Figure 6:
A General finite mixture model,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}
’s are parameters for the specific distribution of each cluster. When it is of Gaussian form, the mixture model is known as Gaussian mixture model (GMM), and the parameters
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}
’s include the mean vectors and covariance matrices for the Gaussian distributions.

A typical finite-dimensional mixture model is a hierarchical model shown in Figure
and consists of the following components:

•

N random variables corresponding to observations
\mathcal{X}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
, where each assumed to be distributed according to a mixture of
K
components, with each component belonging to the same parametric family of distributions (e.g., all Gaussian which have conjugate prior as we have shown previously, all Student
t
which is not an exponential family and therefore there is no conjugate prior, etc.), but with different parameters;

•

A set of
K
mixture weight vector
\bm{\pi}=\{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\ldots,\pi_{K}\}
, each of which is a probability (a real number between 0 and 1 inclusively), all of which sum to 1 so that
\bm{\pi}
is in a
(K-1)
-dimensional simplex;

•

A set of
K
parameters, each specifying the parameter of the corresponding mixture component. In many cases, each “parameter” is actually a set of parameters. For example, observations distributed according to a mixture of one-dimensional Gaussian distributions will have a mean parameter and a variance parameter for each component. And its multivariate version will have a mean vector and covariance matrix for each component.

Specifically, assume we have
N
observations
\mathcal{X}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
sampled i.i.d., from a finite mixture distribution with density

p(\bm{x}|\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\Phi(\bm{x}|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}),

(57)

with
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}\in\Gamma
,
\Gamma
is the metric space of the parameter for some kernel functions,
\Phi
is the kernel function of each component, and
K
is finite and known. We wish to make (Bayesian) inference for the model parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\{\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma}\}
. The likelihood is given by

p(\mathcal{X}|\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\Phi(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}),

(58)

which is given by
K^{N}
terms. This implies a large computational cost for even a not very large sample size,
N
.

Bayesian Approaches VS EM Algorithm

In this article, we use Bayesian inference to do the calculation. However, an alternative frequentist approach exists to handle clustering based on mixture model which is known as the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm where the parameters of the mixture model are usually estimated into a maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) framework by maximizing the observed data likelihood, i.e., the mixture model parameters
\{\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma}\}
can be estimated by maximizing the observed data likelihood in Equation (
). The EM algorithm is advanced in the sense of allowing for different size, shapes, and orientations among the clusters. However, it comes with some limitations that we can overcome with the Bayesian approach. For example, the Bayesian approach will eventually reach the target distribution, even if it takes some time. The EM algorithm estimator runs the risk of getting stuck in a local maximum if present
(
Stephens 1997
;
Fraley and Raftery 2007
)
. In addition, the method only outputs point estimates , and produces no estimates concerning the
uncertainty of the parameters
. However, in Bayesian inference approaches, these problems can be avoided by replacing the MLE by the maximum a posterior (MAP) estimation, i.e., a MAP estimation (Bayesian) framework by maximizing the posterior parameter distribution, that is,
p(\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma}|\mathcal{X})\propto p(\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma})p(\mathcal{X}|\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
, where
p(\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
is a chosen prior distribution on the model parameters
\{\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma}\}
, and
p(\mathcal{X}|\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma})
is the likelihood of the data under the mixture model. This is namely achieved by introducing a regularization over the model parameters via prior parameter distributions , which are assumed to be uniform distributed in the case of MLE. And the Bayesian approaches generate point estimates for all variables as well as associated uncertainty in the form of the whole estimates’ posterior distribution.

In order to simplify the likelihood, we can introduce latent variables
z_{i}
such that:

(\bm{x}_{i}|z_{i}=k)\sim\Phi(\bm{x}|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k})\quad\mathrm{and}\quad p(z_{i}=k)=\pi_{k}.

(59)

These auxiliary variables allow us to identify the mixture component from which each observation has been generated. Therefore, for each sample of data
\mathcal{X}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
, we assume a missing/latent data set
\bm{z}=\{z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{N}\}
, which provides the labels indicating the mixture components from which the observations have been generated. Using this missing data set, the likelihood simplifies to

\displaystyle p(\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma}|\mathcal{X},\bm{z})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\pi_{z_{i}}\Phi(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{z_{i}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}^{N_{k}}\left[\prod_{i:z_{i}=k}\Phi(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k})\right],

(60)

where
N_{k}
is the count of component
k
in
\bm{z}
, i.e.,
N_{k}=\#\{z_{i}=k\}
, and
N=\sum_{k=1}^{K}N_{k}
.

Considering the finite mixture model in Equation (
), a Bayesian approach
is completed by choosing priors for the number of components
K
, the probability weights
\bm{\pi}
, and the component-specific parameters
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\{\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{K}\}
.
Typically,
K
is assigned a Poisson or multinomial prior, or
K
can be chosen with an upper bound of mixture components in an over-fitting mixture model setting,
\bm{\pi}
is assigned a Dirichlet
(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
prior with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\}
, and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}\sim P0
independently, with
P0
often chosen to be conjugate to the kernel
\Phi
. As an example, when
\Phi
is the multivariate Gaussian kernel/distribution and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
is a matrix containing mean vector and covariance matrix, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\}
, a normal-inverse-Wishart prior can be assigned to
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. This mixture model is often referred as the Gaussian mixture model (GMM). We then introduce the mathematical details of this setting in the following sections.

Model-Based Clustering VS Deterministic Clustering

This kind of model-based clustering arised from the Gaussian mixture model has several advantages compared to traditional, deterministic clustering methods (such as k-means). Deterministic methods use different measures between objects, and between objects and centroids, to create cohesive and homogeneous groups. However, they assume equal structure among clusters, and thus cannot handle clusters of different shapes, sizes and directions. Model-based clustering is better able to handle overlapping groups by taking into account cluster membership probabilities in these areas.

10
Bayesian finite Gaussian mixture model

This section is primarily based on
(
Murphy 2012
;
Kamper 2013
;
Lu 2017c
;
Lu 2017a
;
Lu et al. 2018
;
Franzén 2006
)
.

10.1
Background

We present a background of Bayesian finite Gaussian mixture model (GMM) here, also the background can be extended to the situation of infinite Gaussian mixture model. In our case, data is assumed to come from a mixture model of
K
distributions, where each distribution represents a cluster. All clusters have a multivariate Gaussian distribution, but each with its specific mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}
. Along with the mean vectors and covariance matrices, the probabilities for each cluster, and the probabilities of a single observation is belonging to a given cluster, are estimated. Assume we have
N
observations
\mathcal{X}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
sampled i.i.d., from a finite mixture distribution with density

p(\bm{x}|\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}),

(61)

with
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
, and
K
being finite and known. We wish to make Bayesian inference for the model parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\{\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\}
. The likelihood is,

p(\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\mathcal{X})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}).

(62)

10.2
Bayesian finite Gaussian mixture model

We will work with the following definition of Bayesian finite Gaussian mixture model

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{i}|z_{i},\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{z_{i}},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{z_{i}})

(63)

\displaystyle z_{i}|\bm{\pi}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Discrete}(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{K})

\displaystyle\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{NIW}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle\bm{\pi}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}),

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}\}
is a hyper-parameter to generate the probability vector
\bm{\pi}
, and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is a hyper-parameter to generate mean vectors and covariance matrices for multivariate Gaussian distributions.
Using the introduced latent variables
z_{i}
’s, the Bayesian finite Gaussian mixture model is illustrated in Figure
, where hyper-parameters are denoted in green cycles. For each observed data vector
\mathbf{x}_{i}
, we have a latent variable
z_{i}\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
indicating which of the K components
\mathbf{x}_{i}
belongs to. Using this latent variable by
\pi_{k}
=
p
(
z_{i}=k
), we indicate the prior probability that
\mathbf{x}_{i}
belongs to component
k
. Given
z_{i}=k
,
\mathbf{x}_{i}
is generated by the
k^{\textit{th}}
Gaussian mixture component with mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}
.

(a)

General mixture model

(b)

A Bayesian finite GMM

Figure 7:
A Bayesian finite GMM (compared with general mixture models that are not necessarily Gaussian mixture model). Hyper-parameters
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
are denoted in green cycles. Assume a Dirichlet prior for the probability vector
p(\bm{\pi}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})
(i.e., the mixture weights). And a NIW prior for the parameters in multivariate Gaussian distributions.

Here we use a Dirichlet distribution/prior for
p(\bm{\pi}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})
since Dirichlet distribution is a conjugate prior for the multinomial distribution as introduced in Section
. The upper left of the Figure
shows that we use a Dirichlet distribution as a prior over the mixture weights
\bm{\pi}=\{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\ldots,\pi_{K}\}
:

p(\bm{\pi}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\bm{\pi}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}).

(64)

If using hyperprior (that is, a prior over a prior) on Dirichlet distribution, we represent the hyper-parameter of the hyperprior as
a,b
. We will give the detail of hyperprior in later sections.

For the mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}
of each of the K Gaussian mixture components, again we use a NIW distribution with hyper-parameters
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\bm{m}_{0},\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0})
:

p(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}|\boldsymbol{\beta})=\mathrm{NIW}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}|\bm{m}_{0},\kappa_{0},\nu_{0},\bm{S}_{0}).

(65)

We use NIW as the prior of Gaussian component since the NIW is fully conjugate to the multivariate Gaussian likelihood as introduced in Section
.

10.3
Inference by uncollapsed Gibbs sampling

The most widely used posterior inference methods in Bayesian inference models are Markoc Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods as discussed in Section
. The idea of MCMC methods is to define a Markov chain on the hidden variables that has the posterior as its equilibrium distribution
(
Andrieu et al. 2003
)
. By drawing samples from this Markov chain, one eventually obtains samples from the posterior. A simple form of MCMC sampling is Gibbs sampling, where the Markov chain is constructed by considering the conditional distribution of each hidden variable given the others and the observations.

Figure 8:
The Markov blanket of a directed acyclic graphical model. In a Bayesian network, the Markov blanket of node
A
includes its
parents, children and the other parents of all of its children
. That is, the nodes in the cycle are in the Markov blanket of node
A
. The figure is from wikipedia page of Markov blanket.

To do Gibbs sampling, we need to derive the conditional posterior distributions for each parameters conditioned on all the other parameters
p(\theta_{i}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{-i},\mathcal{X})
, where
\mathcal{X}
is again the set of
N
data points and
\theta_{i}
’s are the variables for which we want to sample the distributions. But for a graphical model, this conditional distribution is a function only of the nodes in the Markov blanket. For the finite Gaussian mixture model shown in Figure
, which is a directed acyclic graphical (DAG) model, the Markov blanket of a node includes
the parents, the children, and the co-parents

(
Jordan and Bishop 2004
)
, as shown in Figure
. The Markov blanket of node A is all nodes in the cycle.

An Example on the Markov Blanket

This might be mysterious at first glance. Suppose we want to sample
z_{i}
’s for the distribution of it. From Figure
, we find its parents, children, and coparents are
\{\bm{\pi}\}
,
\{\mathcal{X}\}
, and
\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\}
, respectively. Therefore the conditional distribution of
z_{i}
only depends on the three pairs of parameters:

p(z_{i}=k|-)=p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\bm{\pi},\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\},\{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\},\mathcal{X}).

More specifically, from this graphical representation, we can find the Markov blanket for each parameter in the finite Gaussian mixture model, and then figure out their conditional posterior distributions to be derived:

\displaystyle p(z_{i}=k|-)

\displaystyle=p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\bm{\pi},\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\},\{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\},\mathcal{X})

(66)

\displaystyle p(\bm{\pi}|-)

\displaystyle=p(\bm{\pi}|\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

(67)

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}|-)

\displaystyle=p(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}|\mathcal{X},\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\beta}).

(68)

In other words, Gibbs sampler moves the chain forward by one step as follows:

•

Sample the cluster assignment
z_{i}
for each observation from Equation (
66
) which is known as the
conditional distribution of assignment
;

•

Sample the mixture weights
\bm{\pi}
for each cluster from Equation (
67
), which is known as the
conditional distribution of mixture weights
;

•

Sample the cluster mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
for each cluster from Equation (
68
) which is known as the
conditional distribution of cluster parameters
.

10.3.1
Conditional distribution of assignment

As discussed, the conditional distribution of assignment for each observation is given by

\displaystyle p(z_{i}=k|-)

\displaystyle=p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\bm{\pi},\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\},\{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\},\mathcal{X})

(69)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\bm{\pi},\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\},\{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\},\mathcal{X})}{\cancel{p(\mathbf{z}_{-i},\bm{\pi},\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\},\{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\},\mathcal{X})}}

\displaystyle\propto p(\bm{z},\bm{\pi},\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\},\{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\},\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle=\prod_{n=1}^{N}\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left[\pi_{k}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{n}|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k})\right]^{\delta(z_{n},k)}

\displaystyle\propto\pi_{k}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}),

which comes from the fact that cluster assignements are conditionally independent given the cluster weights and paramters and
\delta(z_{n},k)=1
if
z_{n}=k
and
\delta(z_{n},k)=0
if
z_{n}\neq k
. This equation intuitively makes sense: data point
i
is more likely to be in cluster
k
if
k
is itself probable (
\pi_{k}\gg 0
or
\pi_{k}>\pi_{l}
for
k\neq l
) and
x_{i}
has large probability in the
k^{th}
component (i.e., the probability of
\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k})
is large).

In Gibbs sampling, for each data point
i
, we can compute
p(z_{i}=k|-)
by
\pi_{k}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k})
from the above deduction for each of cluster
k
. These values are the unnormalized parameters to a discrete distribution (since we use proportional distribution in the above deduction) from which we can sample assignments by normalizing it. Let
o_{m}=\pi_{m}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{m})
, for
m\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, the probability to output
z_{i}=k
is

\frac{o_{k}}{\sum_{m=1}^{K}o_{m}}.

10.3.2
Conditional distribution of mixture weights

We can similarly derive the conditional distributions of mixture weights by an application of Bayes’ theorem. Instead of updating each component of
\bm{\pi}
separately, we update them together (this is also known as the
blocked Gibbs sampling
).

\displaystyle p(\bm{\pi}|-)

\displaystyle=p(\bm{\pi}|\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

(70)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(\bm{\pi},\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{\cancel{p(\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\alpha})}}

\displaystyle\propto p(\bm{\pi},\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\cancel{p(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}p(\bm{\pi}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})p(\bm{z}|\bm{\pi},\cancel{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}),

where
p(\bm{\pi}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\bm{\pi}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})\propto\prod_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}
, and
p(\bm{z}|\bm{\pi},\cancel{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
can be seen as a multinomial distribution
p(\bm{z}|\bm{\pi},\cancel{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\mathrm{Multinomial}_{K}(\bm{z}|N,\bm{\pi})\propto\prod_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}^{N_{k}}
, where
N_{k}
is the number of
z_{i}
’s assigned in cluster
k
. From Section
, therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle p(\bm{\pi}|-)

\displaystyle=p(\bm{\pi}|\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

(71)

\displaystyle\propto\prod_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}\prod_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}^{N_{k}}

\displaystyle\propto\mathrm{Dirichlet}(N_{1}+\alpha_{1},N_{2}+\alpha_{2},\ldots,N_{K}+\alpha_{K}).

10.3.3
Conditional distribution of cluster parameters

Finally, we need to compute the conditional distribution for the cluster means and covariance matrices:

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}|-)

\displaystyle=p(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}|\mathcal{X},\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\beta})

(72)

\displaystyle=p(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}|\mathcal{X}_{k},\boldsymbol{\beta})

where
\mathcal{X}_{k}
is the data points in
k^{th}
cluster, and this equation can be easily calculated from Equation (
).

The pseudo code for uncollapsed Gibbs sampler for a finite Gaussian mixture model is given by Algorithm
.

input :
Choose an initial
\bm{z}

for

T
iterations

do

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

N

do

Remove
\mathbf{x}_{i}
’s statistics from component
z_{i}
;

Calculate weights
p(\bm{\pi}|\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\mathrm{Dirichlet}(N_{1}+\alpha_{1},N_{2}+\alpha_{2},\ldots,N_{K}+\alpha_{K})
;

for

k\leftarrow 1

to

K

do

Calculate Gaussian parameters for each cluster:
p(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}|\mathcal{X}_{k},\boldsymbol{\beta})
;

Calculate
p(z_{i}=k|-)\propto\pi_{k}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k})
;

end for

Sample
k_{new}
from
\frac{o_{k}}{\sum_{m}^{K}o_{m}}
after normalizing ;

Add
\mathbf{x}_{i}
’s statistics to the component
z_{i}=k_{new}
;

end for

end for

Algorithm 1

Uncollapsed Gibbs sampler for a finite Gaussian mixture model

10.4
Inference by collapsed Gibbs sampling

Since we choose
p(\bm{\pi}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})
and
p(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}|\boldsymbol{\beta})
to be conjugate, we are able to analytically integrate out the model parameters
\bm{\pi}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}
and only sample the component assignments
\bm{z}
. This is known as a
collapsed Gibbs sampler
, and the discussion about this Gibbs sampler can be found in
(
Neal 2000
;
Murphy 2012
)
and many other articles. The collapsed Gibbs sampler is done as follows:

\displaystyle p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{p(z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})}{\cancel{p(\mathbf{z}_{-i},\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})}}

(73)

\displaystyle\propto p(z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\mathcal{X},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=p(z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})p(\mathcal{X}|z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\cancel{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})\cancel{p(\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})}p(\mathcal{X}|z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\cancel{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle\varpropto p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\cancel{\boldsymbol{\beta}})p(\mathcal{X}|z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\cancel{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha})p(\mathbf{x}_{i}|\mathcal{X}_{-i},z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\beta})p(\mathcal{X}_{-i}|\cancel{z_{i}=k},\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle\varpropto p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha})p(\mathbf{x}_{i}|\mathcal{X}_{-i},z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha})p(\mathbf{x}_{i}|\mathcal{X}_{k,-i},\boldsymbol{\beta}),

where
\mathcal{X}_{k,-i}
is the set of data points assigned to component
k
without taking
\mathbf{x}_{i}
into account.

Typically the
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
hyper-parameter is set to
\alpha_{k}=\alpha=\alpha_{+}/K
(termed as
standard setting of Dirichlet distribution
, also known as a
symmetrical Dirichlet prior
). For this setting we also have
\alpha_{+}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\alpha_{k}
. In the following section, we will give the solutions and proofs for both the unsymmetrical and symmetrical Dirichlet prior. We give the expressions for the first and second terms on the right hand side of Equation (
) respectively in the following two sections.

10.4.1
First term:
p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha})

We can express
p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha})
in Equation (
) as follows

\displaystyle p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\frac{p(z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{p(\mathbf{z}_{-i}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=\frac{p(\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})}{p(\mathbf{z}_{-i}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})},

We can find the numerator and denominator of the above equation is marginal
p(\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})
with
N
and
N-1
samples in
\bm{z}
respectively. Thus we can calculate both the numerator and denominator above if we can find an expression for the marginal
p(\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})
with appropriate modification for the observation sets.

By marginalizing out
\bm{\pi}
for the following equation:

\displaystyle p(\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\int_{\bm{\pi}}p(\bm{z}|\bm{\pi})p(\bm{\pi}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})d\bm{\pi}.

Again, the first term in the integrand is from multinomial distribution

\displaystyle p(\bm{z}|\bm{\pi})=\mathrm{Multinomial}_{K}(\bm{z}|N,\bm{\pi})\propto\prod_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}^{N_{k}},

where
N_{k}
is the count of component
k
in
\bm{z}
. And again, the second term in the integrand is given in Equation (
):
