11.10.8
Digits 1-4

In this experiment, we cluster 1000 and 3000 digits of the classes 1 to 4 in MNIST data set
(
LeCun et al. 2010
)
, where the four clusters are approximate equally distributed. From cross validation on a different set of 1000 samples, we obtain the power value
r=1.05
. The concentration parameter
\alpha
in CRP-Oracle is calculated as 0.58 (
N=1000
) and 0.5
(N=3000)
.

Figure
shows the clustering result of all the three methods for
N=3000
. Both CRP and CRP-Oracle seem to over-fit the data by introducing a small cluster (in red), while pCRP gives a cleaner clustering result with four clusters. This comparison is further confirmed by Table
, where the average posterior cluster number in CRP apparently increases when
N
grows to 3000. In contrast, pCRP is closer to the true situation by reducing the over-clustering effect, even compared to CRP-Oracle; see the columns of
K
and
K_{\max}
. All methods lead to similar NMI but pCRP gives lower VI.

(a)

The distribution of Old Faithful Geyser after standardization

(b)

Clustering results using CRP-Oracle, CRP, pCRP and manual clustering.

Figure 23:
Clustering result for Old Faithful Geyser

11.10.9
Old Faithful Geyser

The Old Faithful Geyser data (
N=272
) are widely used to illustrate the performance of clustering algorithms.
We use a test sample of 100 in CV leading to the power value
r=1.11
. We compare all methods on the other 172 data points. A manual clustering that consists of two Gaussian components is viewed as the ground truth. The concentration parameter is 0.39 in CRP-Oracle. Figure
shows the size of each component obtained from all methods and the manual clustering. We can see that there are two mixture components in CRP-Oracle and pCRP, and four mixture components in the CRP method. In this case where the sample size is relatively small, we again see that pCRP successfully suppresses small components and generate results closer to the ground truth than CRP.

11.11
Some issues in finite and infinite Gaussian mixture model

11.11.1
Non-identifiability due to overfitting finite mixture model or infinite mixture model

In finite Gaussian mixture model, when the number of components is unknown, the analyst can intentionally or unintentionally propose an overfitting model, i.e., one with more components than the true component the data come from. And in infinite Gaussian mixture model, the model does not assume the upper bound of the number of components. Thus, the problem of non-identifiability in estimation of overfitted mixture model and infinite mixture model is well known. For example,
(
Frühwirth-Schnatter 2006
)
observed that identifiability will be violated as either one of the component weights is 0 or two of the component parameters are equal.

More precisely, as in Section
, assume we have
N
observations
\mathcal{X}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
sampled i.i.d., from a finite mixture distribution with density

f_{0}(\bm{x}|\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\sum_{k=1}^{K_{0}}\pi_{k}^{0}\Phi(\bm{x}|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}^{0}),

(144)

with
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}\in\Gamma
,
\Gamma
is the metric space of the parameter for some kernel function,
\Phi
is the kernel function of each component and
K_{0}
is the true component number both in finite and finite Gaussian mixture model. We wish to make Bayesian inference for the model parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\{\bm{\pi}^{0},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{0}\}
.
In such cases the model is non-identifiable since all values of the parameter in the form

\boldsymbol{\theta}=\{\pi_{1}^{0},\ldots,\pi_{K_{0}}^{0},0;\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{0},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{K_{0}}^{0},\boldsymbol{\gamma}\},

(145)

for all
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\Gamma
, and all values of the parameter in the form
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\{\pi_{1}^{0},\ldots,\pi_{j},\ldots,\pi_{K_{0}}^{0},\pi_{K_{0}+1};

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{1}^{0},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{K_{0}}^{0},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{j}^{0}\}
with
\pi_{j}+\pi_{K_{0}+1}=\pi_{j}^{0}
satisfy
f_{0}=f_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. As stated in
(
Rousseau and Mengersen 2011
)
, this non-identifiability is much more tough to deal with than the non-identifiability corresponding to permutation of the labels in the mixture representation. Interesting readers can refer to
(
Rousseau and Mengersen 2011
)
for more details and the references there in.

11.11.2
Read on

If you are interested in a more through understanding of the hyperprior in mixture model,
(
Rasmussen 1999
)
gives some ideas how we can put hyperprior on NIW such that release the burden to select hype-parameter
\boldsymbol{\beta}
for NIW.

11.12
Pruning methods for Dirichlet process mixture model

Further to power Chinese restaurant process, we introduce some pruning methods for Dirichlet proces mixture model.

As a recap, some problem of DP mixture models have been brought about when apply them to practical problems. For example, they always produce more components than that the real data should have. The small mixture components are mainly caused by noise. Some approaches have been proposed for solving this problem. In
(
McCullagh et al. 2008
)
, an upper bound of the number of components is fixed in advance to limit the number in modeling. In
(
Escobar and West 1995
)
, components with little data points are simply discarded, and these data points are reassigned to other existing components. However, this two methods based on simple upper bound or thresholds can not be directly used for real world data, because when you choose a larger bound, DP mixture models can still results in small clusters, and choosing the best thresholds is usually difficult. In this thread, we focus on how to shrink small clusters during sampling.

11.12.1
Constrained sampling (cSampling)

During Gibbs sampling, remove small number of clusters every
s
(e.g., 20) iterations, reassign these data to big clusters by the probability of each cluster. Further extension on cSampling is that when we reassign these small clusters, we can use the assignment method similar to K-means. However, the basic idea is the same.

Important points:

•

Different to the method in
(
Escobar and West 1995
)
, our cSampling method does not need to choose best threshold to discard unuseful clusters. A small threshold is good. It aims to remove very small clusters that can be easily recognized as “noise”.

•

When we remove small cluster during sampling, it will have influence on later sampling iterations. That is where the name “constrained” come from.

11.12.2
loss based sampling (lSampling)

In
(
Kulis and Jordan 2011
)
, the authors introduce an algorithm called DP-means. We here briefly review DP-means. The authors considered asymptotic behavior of DP mixture models, obtaining a hard clustering algorithm that behaves similarly to K-means with the exception that a new cluster is formed whenever a point is farther than
distance
away from existing cluster centroid. The
distance
is very hard to decide, the authors used cross-validation to decide it. However, this violates the setting of unsupervised learning.

Inspired by DP-means, when we doing sampling during DP mixture models, we can shrink out small cluster by some metric, for example: marginal of data and component assignment
p(\mathcal{X},\bm{z}|\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta})
, where
\alpha
is the concentration parameter on Dirichlet Process,
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is the prior parameter on kernel (see Section
). And also we can use inertia, or so called the within-cluster sum of squares criterion (see Section
). Again, during Gibbs sampling, remove small number of clusters every
s
(e.g., 20) iterations by applying to the chosen metric, i.e., if removing the small clusters will get smaller loss, we remove, otherwise, we keep them as they are. In our proposal, we recommend to use the following loss function

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sqrt{\sum_{j:j\in C_{k}}^{N_{k}}||\bm{x}_{j}-\overline{\bm{x}}_{k}||^{2}},

(146)

where
C_{k}
is the data samples in the
k^{th}
cluster. The reason we use a square root over each cluster is that it can overcome identifiablity issue. One can imagine that if two clusters have same center value, the square root operation will force the two clusters into one cluster. This idea on the loss function comes from
(
Petralia et al. 2012
)
, in which case they put a “repulsive” prior on the mixture components, thus overcomes identifiability issue in some sense.

11.12.3
Posterior inference

Although the proposed cSampling and lSampling is generic, we focus on its application in Gaussian mixture models for concreteness. Again, we develop a collapsed Gibbs sampling algorithm
(
Neal 2000
)
for posterior computation.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be the observations, assumed to follow a mixture of multivariate Gaussian distributions. We use a conjugate normal-inverse-Wishart (NIW) prior
p(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\boldsymbol{\beta})
for the mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
in each multivariate Gaussian component, where
\boldsymbol{\beta}
consists of all the hyperparameters in NIW. A key quantity in a collapsed Gibbs sampler is the probability of each customer
i
sitting with table
k
:
p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\mathcal{X},\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta})
, where
\mathbf{z}_{-i}
are the seating assignments of all the other customers and
\alpha
is the concentration parameter in CRP. This probability is calculated as follows:

\displaystyle p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\mathcal{X},\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle\varpropto p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\alpha,\cancel{\boldsymbol{\beta}})p(\mathcal{X}|z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\cancel{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})

(147)

\displaystyle=p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\alpha)p(\mathbf{x}_{i}|\mathcal{X}_{-i},z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\beta})p(\mathcal{X}_{-i}|\cancel{z_{i}=k},\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle\varpropto p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\alpha)p(\mathbf{x}_{i}|\mathcal{X}_{-i},z_{i}=k,\mathbf{z}_{-i},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle\varpropto p(z_{i}=k|\mathbf{z}_{-i},\alpha)p(\mathbf{x}_{i}|\mathcal{X}_{k,-i},\boldsymbol{\beta}),

where
\mathcal{X}_{k,-i}
are the observations in table
k
excluding the
i^{th}
observation. Algorithm
and
give the pseudo code of the collapsed Gibbs sampler to implement cSampling and lSampling in Gaussian mixture models.

\bm{z}

11.12.4
Examples

We conduct experiments to demonstrate the main advantages of the proposed pruning sampling methods using both synthetic and real data.

In all experiments, we run the Gibbs sampler 20,000 iterations with a burn-in of 10,000. The sampler is thinned by keeping every 5
th
draw. We use the same concentration parameter
\alpha=1
for both CRP and pruning sampling methods in all scenarios. In addition, we equip CRP with an unfair advantage to match the magnitude of its prior mean
\alpha\log(N)
to the true number of clusters, termed
CRP-Oracle
. In order to measure overall clustering performance, we use normalized mutual information (NMI)
(
McDaid et al. 2013
)
and variation of information (VI)
(
Meilă 2003
)
, which measures the similarity between the true and estimated cluster assignments. Higher NMI and lower VI indicate better performance. If applicable, metrics using the true clustering are calculated to provide an upper bound for all methods, coded as ‘Ground Truth’. The metrics are discussed in Section
. Feel free to skip this section for a first reading.

11.12.5
Simulation experiments

The parameters of the simulations are as follows:

•

Sim 1:
K_{0}=3
,
\bm{\pi}
={0.35, 0.4, 0.25},
\bm{\mu}
={0, 2, 5} and
\bm{\Sigma}
={0.5, 0.5, 1};

•

Sim 2:
K_{0}=2
,
\bm{\pi}
={0.65, 0.35},
\bm{\mu}
={1, 1} and
\bm{\Sigma}
={10, 1};

In practice, we think that a trivial threshold for cSampling is
4\%
, which means that we consider cluster with samples smaller than
4\%
of total samples can be regarded as noise.
For lSampling, we shrink by the proposed loss function every 20 steps.

shows the posterior summary for these tests. We notice that cSampling gives better results than CRP-Oracle but a little bit worse than pCRP. lSampling gives worse results than CRP-Oracle.

12
Some metrics

In order to evaluate the Gibbs sampling procedure and to ensure that mixing is taking place, it is useful to have some metrics to calculate over the sampling iterations. We consider two kinds of metrics, one is label-related, in which case we use the true label of clustering to evaluate the process; the other one is non-label-related, in which case we do not use the true label to evaluate the process.

12.1
Marginal of data and component assignment

12.1.1
In Bayesian finite Gaussian mixture model

Marginal of the data and component assignments
p(\mathcal{X},\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})
is useful for evaluating the Gibbs sampling process since it captures both changes in the likelihood of the data under the current assignments through
p(\mathcal{X}|\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\beta})
, as well as the probability of the current component assignments
p(\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})
. This marginal of data and component assignments can be calculated as follows

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{X},\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=p(\mathcal{X}|\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\beta})p(\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})

(148)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{k=1}^{K}p(\mathcal{X}_{k}|\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)p(\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}),

where
\mathcal{X}_{k}
is the set of data observations assigned to component/cluster
k
. The terms in the product in Equation (
) can each be calculated using Equation (
) and Equation (
77
).

12.1.2
In Bayesian infinite Gaussian mixture model

Similar to the finite case, the marginal of the data and component assignments
p(\mathcal{X},\bm{z}|\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta})
can be used as evaluation of the sampling process

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{X},\bm{z}|\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=p(\mathcal{X}|\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\beta})p(\bm{z}|\alpha)

(149)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{k=1}^{K}p(\mathcal{X}_{k}|\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)p(\bm{z}|\alpha),

The terms in the product in Equation (
) can each be calculated using Equation (
) and Equation (
). The only difference between Equation (
) and Equation (
) is that in Equation (
) the marginal probability of assignments depends on a vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, while in Equation (
), it depends on a scalar
\alpha
. Also in infinite case, the cluster number
K
can increase or decrease at each iteration.

12.2
Mixture likelihood

The marginal likelihood can be used as a metric to evaluate the sampling iterations since it captures both the likelihood of data under current assignment through
p(\mathcal{X}|\bm{z},\boldsymbol{\beta})
, and the probability of the current component assignment
p(\bm{z}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})
. In this sense, an alternative metric can be utilized from Equation (
) after we sample out the distribution parameters
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
’s and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}
’s for multivariate Gaussian distributions:

\displaystyle p(\bm{\pi},\boldsymbol{\gamma}|\mathcal{X},\bm{z})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\pi_{z_{i}}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{z_{i}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}^{N_{k}}\left[\prod_{i:i\in C_{k}}\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{i}|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k})\right],

where
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{k}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{k}\}
, and
C_{k}
is the data samples in the
k^{th}
cluster. By evaluating with this metric, the mixture model tends to select maximum likelihood estimates.

12.3
Inertia

Inertia , or within-cluster sum-of-squares is mostly used in K-means, in which it aims to choose centroids that minimize the inertia function

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j:j\in C_{k}}^{N_{k}}||\bm{x}_{j}-\overline{\bm{x}}_{k}||^{2}.

(150)

where
C_{k}
is the data samples in the
k^{th}
cluster and
\overline{\bm{x}}_{k}
is the mean vector for cluster
k
. This is exactly the same metric used in K-means. Inertia is not a normalized metric, so we just know that lower values are better and zero is optimal. But in very high-dimensional spaces, Euclidean distances tend to become inflated (this is an instance of the so-called “curse of dimensionality”).

12.4
Squared inertia

We propose the following loss function

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sqrt{\sum_{j:j\in C_{k}}^{N_{k}}||\bm{x}_{j}-\overline{\bm{x}}_{k}||^{2}}.

(151)

where
C_{k}
is the data samples in the
k^{th}
cluster and
\overline{\bm{x}}_{k}
is the mean vector for cluster
k
. The square root has an important impact in favoring a smaller nunber of clusters; for example, inducing a price to be paid for introducing two clusters with the same mean. One can imagine that if two clusters have same center value, the square root operation will force the two clusters into one cluster. For example, if we have the loss value for two clusters 100 and 30 respectively. If we use this square root operation, we will get
\sqrt{100}+\sqrt{30}>\sqrt{100+30}
, thus favoring small cluster number. However, if we do not use square root operation, we will get
(100)+(30)=(100+30)
.

12.5
Label-related metrics

With the increasing popularity of algorithms for clustering, given a set of true cluster assignments, and the set of clusters found by an algorithm, these sets of cluster assignment can be compared to see how similar or different the sets are. We call this as label-related metrics. A normalized measure is desirable in many contexts, for example assigning a value of 0 where the two sets are totally dissimilar, and 1 where they are identical
(
McDaid et al. 2013
)
. We first introduce two un-normalized measures, and a normalized measure is described, all of which come from
information theory
, a field has deep links to statistics and machine learning.
A Python implementation is available online.

Figure 25:
Venn diagram illustrating the relation between information entropies, mutual information and variation of information. The area containing both circles is the joint entropy
H(X,Y)
. The circle on the left (green and grey) is the individual entropy
H(X)
, with the green being the conditional entropy
H(X|Y)
. The circle on the right (purple and grey) is
H(Y)
, with the purple being
H(Y|X)
. The grey is the mutual information
I(X;Y)
. Figure is due to wikipedia.

12.5.1
Mutual information and variation of information

Formally, the mutual information of two discrete random variables
X
and
Y
can be defined as:

MI(X;Y)=\sum_{y\in Y}\sum_{x\in X}p(x,y)\log\left(\frac{p(x,y)}{p(x)p(y)}\right),

(152)

where
p(x,y)
is the joint probability distribution function of
X
and
Y
, and
p(x)
and
p(y)
are the marginal probability distribution functions of
X
and
Y
respectively .

Then the variation of information between the two discrete random variables is defined as

VI(X;Y)=-\sum_{y\in Y}\sum_{x\in X}p(x,y)\left[\log\frac{p(x,y)}{p(x)}+\log\frac{p(x,y)}{p(y)}\right].

(153)

Noted that unlike the mutual information, however, the variation of information is a true metric, in that it obeys the triangle inequality.

The relationship between mutual information and variation of information can be shown in Figure
, where the definition of the entropy of a random variable
X
is
H(X)=\sum_{x\in X}p(x)\log p(x)
. From which we have the relationship between mutual information and variation of information:
VI(X;Y)=H(X)+H(Y)-2MI(X;Y)
.

In clustering, each clustering algorithm
C=\{C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{K}\}
defines the probability distribution
P_{C}

P_{C}(k)=\frac{n_{k}}{N},

(154)

where
n_{k}
is the number of points in the
k^{th}
cluster
C_{k}
and
N
is the total number of points in the data set. Different clustering algorithms can determine different number of clusters.

For any clustering distributions
P_{C_{1}}=(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{n})
and
P_{C_{2}}=(q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{m})
, define the probability distribution
P_{C_{1}}
and
P_{C_{2}}
and joint probability distribution
R(i,j)

\displaystyle P_{C_{1}}(i)

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{i}}{N},

(155)

\displaystyle P_{C_{2}}(j)

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{j}}{N},

\displaystyle R(i,j)

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{i,j}}{N}\triangleq\frac{|n_{i}\cap m_{j}|}{N}.

where
|n_{i}\cap m_{j}|
is the number of observations that is both in cluster
i
of
C_{1}
and cluster
j
of
C_{2}
.
Thus, in clustering algorithms, we set
p(x,y)=R(i,j)
,
p(x)=P_{C_{1}}(i)
and
p(y)=P_{C_{2}}(j)
. Then we arrive at the definition of mutual information (MI) and variation of information (VI)
(
Meilă 2003
)
in clustering

MI(C_{1};C_{2})=\sum_{i,j}\frac{N_{i,j}}{N}\log\frac{\frac{N_{i,j}}{N}}{\frac{n_{i}}{N}\cdot\frac{m_{j}}{N}}=\sum_{i,j}\frac{N_{i,j}}{N}\log\frac{N\cdot N_{i,j}}{n_{i}\cdot m_{j}},

(156)

and

VI(C_{1};C_{2})=-\sum_{i,j}\frac{N_{i,j}}{N}[\log\frac{\frac{N_{i,j}}{N}}{\frac{n_{i}}{N}}+\log\frac{\frac{N_{i,j}}{N}}{\frac{m_{j}}{N}}]=-\sum_{i,j}\frac{N_{i,j}}{N}[\log\frac{N_{i,j}}{n_{i}}+\log\frac{N_{i,j}}{m_{j}}].

(157)

12.5.2
Normalized mutual information

A normalized measure is desirable in many contexts, for example assigning a value of 0 where the two sets are totally dissimilar, and 1 where they are identical.
From Figure
, we find that the mutual information
MI(X;Y)<[H(X)+H(Y)]/2
. Thus we normalized the mutual information to get the normalized mutual information

NMI(X;Y)=\frac{MI(X;Y)}{[H(X)+H(Y)]/2},

(158)

and in clustering, we have

NMI(C_{1};C_{2})=\frac{MI(C_{1};C_{2})}{[H(C_{1})+H(C_{2})]/2}.

(159)

In most situations, we need to compare the clustering algorithm to a true clustering situation, in which case we just set
C_{1}
to be the true clustering label. And thus, we expect the higher the mutual information (or normalized mutual information) the better; and the lower variation of information the better.

Appendix A
Deriving the Dirichlet distribution

A.1
Derivation

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{K}
be i.i.d., random variables drawn from the Gamma distribution such that
X_{k}\sim\mathrm{Ga}(\alpha_{k},1)
for
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
. The joint p.d.f., of
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{K}
is given by

f_{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{K}}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{K})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\prod_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}\exp(-x_{k}),&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}x_{k}\geq 0.\\
&0,&\mathrm{\,\,if\,\,}\text{otherwise}.\end{aligned}\right.

Define variables
Y_{k}
’s as follows

\displaystyle Y_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{k}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}X_{k}},\qquad\forall\,\,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K-1\}

(160)

\displaystyle Y_{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{K}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}X_{k}}=1-\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}Y_{k},

and

Z_{K}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}X_{k}.

(161)

Let
\bm{X}=[X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{K}]
,
\bm{Y}=[Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{K-1},Z_{K}]
,
\bm{x}=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{K}]
, and
\bm{y}=[y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{K-1},z_{K}]
.
By multidimensional transformation of variables, we have

f_{\bm{Y}}(\bm{y})=f_{\bm{X}}(g^{-1}(\bm{y}))\left|\det\left[J_{g^{-1}}(\bm{y})\right]\right|,

where

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
\vdots\\
x_{K-1}\\
x_{K}\end{bmatrix}=g^{-1}(\bm{y})=g^{-1}\left(\begin{bmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{K-1}\\
z_{K}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}y_{1}\cdot z_{K}\\
y_{2}\cdot z_{K}\\
\vdots\\
y_{K-1}\cdot z_{K}\\
y_{K}\cdot z_{K}\end{bmatrix},

and the Jacobian matrix is given by

\displaystyle J_{g^{-1}}(\bm{y})

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}}g_{1}^{-1}(\bm{y})&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{K-1}}g_{1}^{-1}(\bm{y})&\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{K}}g_{1}^{-1}(\bm{y})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{1}}g_{K}^{-1}(\bm{y})&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{K-1}}g_{K}^{-1}(\bm{y})&\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{K}}g_{K}^{-1}(\bm{y})\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}z_{K}&0&\cdots&0&y_{1}\\
0&z_{K}&\cdots&0&y_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&z_{K}&y_{K-1}\\
-z_{K}&-z_{k}&\cdots&-z_{K}&(1-\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}y_{k})\end{bmatrix}=z_{K}^{K-1}.

This implies the joint p.d.f, of
\bm{Y}
is

f_{\bm{Y}}(\bm{y})=f_{\bm{X}}(g^{-1}(\bm{y}))z_{K}^{K-1}=\frac{y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}y_{2}^{\alpha_{2}-1}\ldots y_{K-1}^{\alpha_{K-1}-1}y_{K}^{\alpha_{K}-1}}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\exp(-z_{K})z_{K}^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{K}-1}.

We realize that the righthand size of above equation is proportional to a p.d.f. of Gamma distribution and

\int\exp(-z_{K})z_{K}^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{K}-1}dz_{K}=\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{K}).

Let
\alpha_{+}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{K}
,
this implies

f(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{K-1})=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\prod_{k=1}^{K}y_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}.

We notice that
Y_{k}
’s are defined that
0<Y_{k}<1
for all
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, and
\sum_{k=1}^{K}Y_{k}=1
. This implies the above equation is the p.d.f., of the Dirichlet distribution. The construction shown above can be utilized to generate random variables from the Dirichlet distribution.

A.2
Properties of Dirichlet distribution

Suppose
\bm{Y}=[Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{K}]\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{K}]
, we here show the moments and properties of the Dirichlet distribution.

Mean of Dirichlet distribution

Write out the expectation:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}[Y_{1}]

\displaystyle=\int\cdots\int y_{1}\cdot\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\bm{y}|\boldsymbol{\alpha})dy_{1}dy_{2}\cdots dy_{K}

\displaystyle=\int\cdots\int y_{1}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\prod_{k=1}^{K}y_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}dy_{1}dy_{2}\cdots dy_{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\int\cdots\int y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}+1-1}\prod_{k=2}^{K}y_{k}^{\alpha_{k}-1}dy_{1}dy_{2}\cdots dy_{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+1)\prod_{k=2}^{K}\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{+}+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1})}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{+}+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{+}},

where the last equality comes from the fact that
\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x)
.

Variance of Dirichlet distribution

Write out the variance
\mathrm{Var}[Y_{i}]=\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}^{2}]-\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}]^{2}
. Similarly from the proof of the mean, we have

\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}^{2}]=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{+}+2)}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{i}+2)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}=\frac{(\alpha_{i}+1)\alpha_{i}}{(\alpha_{+}+1)\alpha_{+}}.

This implies

\mathrm{Var}[Y_{i}]=\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}^{2}]-\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}]^{2}=\frac{(\alpha_{i}+1)\alpha_{i}}{(\alpha_{+}+1)\alpha_{+}}-(\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{+}})^{2}=\frac{\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{i})}{\alpha_{+}^{2}(\alpha_{+}+1)}.

Covariance of Dirichlet distribution

Write out the covariance
\mathrm{Cov}[Y_{i}Y_{j}]=\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}Y_{j}]-\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}]\mathrm{E}[Y_{j}]
.
Again, similarly from the proof of the mean, for
i\neq j
, we have

\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}Y_{j}]=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{+})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{+}+2)}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{i}+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{j}+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{j})}=\frac{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{+}(\alpha_{+}+1)}.

This implies

\mathrm{Cov}[Y_{i}Y_{j}]=\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}Y_{j}]-\mathrm{E}[Y_{i}]\mathrm{E}[Y_{j}]=\frac{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{+}(\alpha_{+}+1)}-\frac{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{+}^{2}}=\frac{-\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{+}^{2}(\alpha_{+}+1)}.

Marginal distribution of
Y_{i}

By definition in Equation (
) and Equation (
), we have

Z_{K}-X_{i}\sim\mathrm{Ga}(\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{i},1)
.
This implies

Y_{i}=\frac{X_{i}}{Z_{K}}=\frac{X_{i}}{X_{i}+(Z_{K}-X_{i})}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{i}).

which is from the fact about the p.d.f., of two independent Gamma random variables.

Aggregation property

Suppose
[Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{K}]\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}([\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{K}])
, Then, Let
M=Y_{i}+Y_{j}
, it follows that

\displaystyle[Y_{1},\ldots Y_{i-1},Y_{i+1},\ldots,Y_{j-1},Y_{j+1},\ldots,Y_{K},M]

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}([\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1},\alpha_{i+1},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1},\alpha_{j+1},\ldots,\alpha_{K},\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}]).

Proof.

We realize that
M\sim\mathrm{Ga}(\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j},1)
. Again by the multidimensional transformation of variables as shown in the beginning of this section, we conclude the result.
∎

The results can be extended to a more general case. If
\{A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{r}\}
is a partition of
\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
, then

\left[\sum_{i\in A_{1}}Y_{i},\sum_{i\in A_{2}}Y_{i},\ldots,\sum_{i\in A_{r}}Y_{i}\right]\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}\left(\left[\sum_{i\in A_{1}}\alpha_{i},\sum_{i\in A_{2}}\alpha_{i},\ldots,\sum_{i\in A_{r}}\alpha_{i}\right]\right).

Condition distribution

Let
Y_{0}=\sum_{k=3}^{K}Y_{i}
and
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{+}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}
, then
[Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{0}]\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}([\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{0}])
. Therefore

f_{Y_{1},Y_{2}}(y_{1},y_{2})=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{0})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1})\Gamma(\alpha_{2})\Gamma(\alpha_{0})}y_{1}^{\alpha_{1}-1}y_{2}^{\alpha_{2}-1}(1-y_{1}-y_{2})^{\alpha_{0}-1}.

Similarly, we have

f_{Y_{2}}(y_{2})=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{0})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{2})\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0})}y_{2}^{\alpha_{2}-1}(1-y_{2})^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}-1}=\mathrm{Beta}(y_{1}|\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}),

which is a p.d.f., of a Beta distribution. Therefore, the conditional p.d.f., of
Y_{1}|Y_{2}=y_{2}
is given by

f_{Y_{1}|Y_{2}=y_{2}}(y_{1}|y_{2})=\frac{f_{Y_{1},Y_{2}}(y_{1},y_{2})}{f_{Y_{2}}(y_{2})}=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1})\Gamma(\alpha_{0})}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{1-y_{2}}\right)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\left(1-\frac{y_{1}}{1-y_{2}}\right)^{\alpha_{0}-1}\frac{1}{1-y_{2}},

which implies

\frac{1}{1-y_{2}}Y_{1}|Y_{2}=y_{2}\sim\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}).

Apply this procedure, we will have

\bm{Y}_{-i}|Y_{i}\sim(1-y_{i})\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\alpha_{-i}),

where
Y_{-i}
is all the
K-1
variables except
Y_{i}
, and similarly for
\alpha_{-i}
.

Appendix B
Cholesky decomposition

Theorem 25

(Cholesky Decomposition)
.

Every positive definite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
can be factored as

\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R},

where
\bm{R}
is an upper triangular matrix with positive diagonal elements. This decomposition is known as
Cholesky decomposition
of
\bm{A}
.
\bm{R}
is known as the
Cholesky factor
or
Cholesky triangle
of
\bm{A}
.

B.1
Existence of the Cholesky decomposition

Before showing the existence of Cholesky decomposition, we need the following definitions and lemmas.

Definition 26

(Positive Definite and Positive Semidefinite)
.

A matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is positive definite if
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}>0
for all nonzero
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
.
And a matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
is positive semidefinite if
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}\geq 0
for all
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}
.

Lemma 27

(Positive Diagonals of Positive Definite Matrices)
.

The diagonal elements of a positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
are all positive.

of Lemma
.

From the definition of positive definite matrix, we have
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}>0
for all nonzero
\bm{x}
. In particular, let
\bm{x}=\bm{e}_{i}
where
\bm{e}_{i}
is the
i
-th unit vector with the
i
-th entry equal to 1 and other entries equal to 0. Then,

\bm{e}_{i}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{e}_{i}=\bm{A}_{ii}>0,\qquad\forall i\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 28

(Schur Complement of Positive Definite Matrices)
.

For any positive definite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, its Schur complement of
\bm{A}_{11}
is
\bm{S}_{n-1}=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}
and it is also positive definite.

Note that the subscript
n-1
of
\bm{S}_{n-1}
means it is of size
(n-1)\times(n-1)
and it is a Schur complement of a
n\times n
positive definite matrix. We will use this notation in the following section.

of Lemma
.

For any nonzero vector
\bm{v}\in\real^{n-1}
, we can construct a vector
\bm{x}\in\real^{n}

\bm{x}=\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\bm{v}\\
\bm{v}\end{bmatrix},

which is nonzero. Then

\displaystyle\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=[-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\qquad\bm{v}^{\top}]\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{11}&\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\bm{v}\\
\bm{v}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=[-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\qquad\bm{v}^{\top}]\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\bm{S}_{n-1}\bm{v}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}_{n-1}\bm{v}.

Since
\bm{A}
is positive definite, we have
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{S}_{n-1}\bm{v}>0
for all nonzero
\bm{v}
. Thus,
\bm{S}
is positive definite.
∎

A word on the Schur complement
:
It can be easily proved that this Schur complement
\bm{S}_{n-1}=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}
is also nonsingular if
\bm{A}
is nonsngular and
\bm{A}_{11}\neq 0
. Similarly, the Schur complement of
\bm{A}_{nn}
in
\bm{A}
is
\bar{\bm{S}}_{n-1}=\bm{A}_{1:n-1,1:n-1}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{nn}}\bm{A}_{1:n-1,n}\bm{A}_{1:n-1,n}^{\top}
which is also positive definite if
\bm{A}
is positive definite. This property can help prove the leading principle minors of positive definite matrices are all positive. See Appendix
for more details.

We then prove the existence of Cholesky decomposition using these lemmas.

of Theorem
: Existence of Cholesky Decomposition
.

For any positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
, we can write out (since
\bm{A}_{11}
is positive)

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{11}&\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\bm{S}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{1}.

where

\bm{R}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\bm{I}\end{bmatrix}.

Since we proved the Schur complement
\bm{S}_{n-1}
is positive definite. We can factor it in the same way

\bm{S}_{n-1}=\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\bm{S}_{n-2}\end{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}.

We then have

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\bm{S}_{n-2}\end{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}.\end{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\bm{S}_{n-2}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\hat{\bm{R}}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\begin{bmatrix}1&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\bm{S}_{n-2}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}\bm{R}_{2}\bm{R}_{1}.

The same formula can be recursively applied. This process gradually continues down to the bottom-right corner giving us the decomposition

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}_{1}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2}^{\top}\cdots\bm{R}_{n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{n}\cdots\bm{R}_{2}\bm{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R},

where
\bm{R}_{1},\bm{R}_{2},\cdots\bm{R}_{n},
are upper triangular matrices with positive diagonal elements and
\bm{R}=\bm{R}_{1}\bm{R}_{2}\cdots\bm{R}_{n}
is also an upper triangular matrix with positive diagonal elements.
∎

The process in the proof can also be used to compute the Cholesky decomposition. In next section, we use another point of view to do the computation.

Corollary 29

(
\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
is PD)
.

For any upper triangular matrix with positive diagonal elements, then

\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}

is positive definite.

of Corollary
.

If an upper triangular matrix
\bm{R}
has positive diagonal, it is full column rank, and the null space of
\bm{R}
is 0. As a result,
\bm{R}\bm{x}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
for any nonzero vector
\bm{x}
. Thus
\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}=||\bm{R}\bm{x}||^{2}>0
for any nonzero vector
\bm{x}
.
∎

This corollary can be extended to any
\bm{R}
with independent columns.

B.2
Computing the Cholesky decomposition

To compute Cholesky decomposition, we write out the equality
\bm{A}=\bm{R}^{\top}\bm{R}
:

\displaystyle\bm{A}=\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{A}_{11}&\bm{A}_{1,2:n}\\
\bm{A}_{2:n,1}&\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{R}_{11}&0\\
\bm{R}_{1,2:n}^{\top}&\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}^{\top}\end{matrix}\right]\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{R}_{11}&\bm{R}_{1,2:n}\\
0&\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}\end{matrix}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{R}_{11}^{2}&\bm{R}_{11}\bm{R}_{1,2:n}\\
\bm{R}_{11}\bm{R}_{1,2:n}^{\top}&\bm{R}_{1,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{1,2:n}+\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}\end{matrix}\right],

which allows to determine the first row of
\bm{R}

\bm{R}_{11}=\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}},\qquad\bm{R}_{1,2:n}=\frac{1}{\bm{R}_{11}}\bm{A}_{1,2:n}.

Let
\bm{A}_{2}=\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}
. The equality
\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}=\bm{R}_{1,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{1,2:n}+\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}
gives out

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{2}=\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\bm{R}_{1,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{1,2:n}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{1,2:n}^{\top}\bm{A}_{1,2:n}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{1,2:n}\qquad(\bm{A}\mbox{ is positive definite}).

\bm{A}_{2}
is the Schur complement of
\bm{A}_{11}
in
\bm{A}
of size
(n-1)\times(n-1)
. And to get
\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}
we must compute the Cholesky decomposition of matrix
\bm{A}_{2}
of
(n-1)\times(n-1)
. Again, this is a recursive algorithm and formulated in Algorithm
.

1:

positive definite matrix
\bm{A}
with size
n\times n
;

2:

Calculate first of
\bm{R}
by
\bm{R}_{11}=\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}},\bm{R}_{1,2:n}=\frac{1}{\bm{R}_{11}}\bm{A}_{1,2:n}
, (
n
flops);

3:

Compute the Cholesky decomposition of the
(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix

\bm{A}_{2}=\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{2:n,2:n}=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{1,2:n},\qquad\text{($n^{2}-n$ flops).}

Algorithm 8

Cholesky Decomposition

Further, this process can be used to determine if a matrix is positive definite or not. If we try to factor a non positive definite matrix, at some point, we will encounter a nonpositive element in entry (1,1) of
\bm{A},\bm{A}_{2},\bm{A}_{3},\cdots
.

Theorem 30
.

Algorithm
requires
\sim(1/3)n^{3}
flops to compute a Cholesky decomposition of an
n\times n
positive definite matrix.

of Theorem
.

Step 1 takes 1 square root and
(n-1)
division which takes
n
flops totally.

For step 2, Note that
\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{1,2:n}=(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1})(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{A}_{11}}}\bm{A}_{1,2:n})=\bm{R}_{1,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{1,2:n}
. If we calculate the complexity directly from the equation in step 2, we will get the same complexity as LU decomposition. But since
\bm{R}_{1,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{1,2:n}
is symmetric, the complexity of
\bm{R}_{1,2:n}^{\top}\bm{R}_{1,2:n}
reduces from
(n-1)\times(n-1)
multiplications to
1+2+\cdots+(n-1)=\frac{n^{2}-n}{2}
multiplications. The cost of matrix division reduces from
(n-1)\times(n-1)
to
1+2+\cdots+(n-1)=\frac{n^{2}-n}{2}
as well. So it costs
n^{2}-n
flops for step 2.

Simple calculation will show the total complexity is
\frac{2n^{3}+3n^{2}+n}{6}
flops which is
(1/3)n^{3}
flops if we keep only the leading term.
∎

Appendix C
Leading Principle Minors of PD Matrices

In Lemma
, we proved for any positive definite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
, it’s Schur complement of
\bm{A}_{11}
is
\bm{S}_{n-1}=\bm{A}_{2:n,2:n}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{11}}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}\bm{A}_{2:n,1}^{\top}
and it is also positive definite.
This is also true for its Schur complement of
\bm{A}_{nn}
, i.e.,
\bm{S}_{n-1}^{\prime}=\bm{A}_{1:n-1,1:n-1}-\frac{1}{\bm{A}_{nn}}\bm{A}_{1:n-1,n}\bm{A}_{1:n-1,n}^{\top}
is also positive definite.

We then claim all the leading principle minors of a positive definite matrix
\bm{A}\in\real^{n\times n}
are positive.

Proof.

We will prove by induction.
Since all the components on the diagonal of positive definite matrices are all positive (see Lemma
). The case for
n=1
is trivial that
\det(\bm{A}_{11})>0
.

Suppose all the leading principle minors for
k\times k
matrices are all positive. If we could prove this is also true for
(k+1)\times(k+1)
matrices, then we complete the proof.

For a
(k+1)\times(k+1)
matrix
\bm{M}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}&\bm{b}\\
\bm{b}^{\top}&d\end{bmatrix}
, where
\bm{A}
is a
k\times k
submatrix. Then its Schur complement of
d
,
\bm{S}_{k}=\bm{A}-\frac{1}{d}\bm{b}\bm{b}^{\top}
is also positive definite and its determinant is positive from the assumption. And
\det(\bm{M})=\det(d)\det(\bm{A}-\frac{1}{d}\bm{b}\bm{b}^{\top})
=

d\cdot\det(\bm{A}-\frac{1}{d}\bm{b}\bm{b}^{\top})>0
, which completes the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Convexity results

We prove that
x\mapsto\Gamma(Kx)/(x^{K-1}[\Gamma(x)]^{K})
is strictly log-convex,
x\mapsto\Gamma(Kx)/[\Gamma(x)]^{K}
is strictly log-concave and the function
x\mapsto\Gamma(x+t)/\Gamma(x)
is also strictly log-concave where
\Gamma(x)
is the Gamma function.

Theorem 31
.

Define

F(x)=\frac{\Gamma(Kx)}{x^{K-1}[\Gamma(x)]^{K}},\quad G(x)=\frac{\Gamma(Kx)}{[\Gamma(x)]^{K}}.

(162)

For
x>0
and
K
is an arbitrary positive integer, the function F is strictly log-convex and the function G is strictly log-concave.

Proof.

Follow from
(
Abramowitz et al. 1966
)
we get
\Gamma(Kx)=(2\pi)^{\frac{1}{2}(1-K)}K^{Kx-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i=0}^{K-1}\Gamma(x+\frac{i}{K})
. Then

\log F(x)=\frac{1}{2}(1-K)\log(2\pi)+(Kx-\frac{1}{2})\log K+\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\log\Gamma(x+\frac{i}{K})-K\log\Gamma(x)-(K-1)\log x

(163)

and

[\log F(x)]^{\prime}=K\log K+\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\Psi(x+\frac{i}{K})-K\Psi(x)-(K-1)x^{-1},

(164)

where
\Psi(x)
is the Digamma function, and

\Psi^{\prime}(x)=\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(x+h)^{2}}.

(165)

Thus

[\log F(x)]^{\prime\prime}=\left[\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\Psi^{\prime}(x+\frac{i}{K})\right]-K\Psi^{\prime}(x)+\frac{K-1}{x^{2}}>0,\quad(x>0).

(166)

The last inequality comes from (
) (also, we can find the derivative of Digamma function is monotone decreasing).
Easily, we can get

\log G(x)=\frac{1}{2}(1-K)\log(2\pi)+(Kx-\frac{1}{2})\log K+\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\log\Gamma(x+\frac{i}{K})-K\log\Gamma(x)

(167)

and

[\log G(x)]^{\prime}=K\log K+\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\Psi(x+\frac{i}{K})-K\Psi(x),

(168)

Thus

[\log G(x)]^{\prime\prime}=\left[\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\Psi^{\prime}(x+\frac{i}{K})\right]-K\Psi^{\prime}(x)<0,\quad(x>0).

(169)

This concludes the theorem.
∎

This theorem is a general case of Theorem 1 in
(
Merkle 1997
)
.

Theorem 32
.

Define

H(x)=\frac{\Gamma(x+t)}{\Gamma(x)}.

(170)

For
x>0
and
t
is a constant that
x+t>0
(or for simplicity we can let
t>0
), the function H is strictly log-concave.

Proof.

We can easily get

\log H(x)=\log\Gamma(x+t)-\log\Gamma(x)

(171)

and

[\log H(x)]^{\prime}=\Psi(x+t)-\Psi(x),

(172)

where
\Psi(x)
is the Digamma function, and

\Psi^{\prime}(x)=\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(x+h)^{2}}.

(173)

Thus

[\log H(x)]^{\prime\prime}=\Psi^{\prime}(x+t)-\Psi^{\prime}(x)<0,\quad(x>0,t>0).

(174)

The last inequality comes from (
) which is monotone decreasing and concludes the theorem.
∎
